#3015. Chiefs-Jets: 5 questions with Gang Green Nation ahead of Week 9

On Sunday, the Kansas City Chiefs face the New York Jets in the early afternoon slot of Week 9 games. We welcome MacGregor Wells of Gang Green Nation— our sister SBNation site covering the Jets— for Five Questions with the Enemy.

Join fellow Chiefs fans to react to every explosive play and game-changing moment in real time.

Download the SB Nation app on iOS or Android.

Download the SB Nation app on your mobile device and join the Gang Green Nation community through Sunday's game to get perspectives from both sides of the Week 9 matchup!

Part of this is always luck. Which team wins a one-score game often comes down to an untimely turnover, a costly penalty, an unlucky bounce, whatever. Things that are going to happen to any team, but happen at the wrong time to losing teams. However, the element that isn't just bad luck for the Jets is bad coaching. The Jets have blown fairly big second-half leads. They have had comebacks come up just short. They have outplayed better teams only to lose late, and they have come out flat against lesser teams they should have beaten. The one stat that people keep on bringing up is a 100 – 6 deficit in the last two minutes of halves under Aaron Glenn's "leadership." I think the numbers have changed slightly since that stat came out, but you get the picture.

That kind of absurd imbalance in two-minute situations is the direct result of poor coaching. The Jets look completely lost in two-minute drills. How can an NFL team be that clueless and that ineffective in crunch time? It's nuts. There are plenty of other coaching issues I could mention, but that one stat kind of encapsulates the general incompetence of the Jets coaching.

It's frustrating because for so many years the Jets simply didn't have the talent to be competitive. This year they have the talent. Oh, don't get me wrong, this is no juggernaut roster. But unlike recent years, the Jets at least have the talent to be competitive in almost every game. They have been competitive. But they continue to lose. Thank you, Aaron Glenn.

Part of this is probably just good luck. Smith has always been a turnover-prone quarterback. He began this season with five straight interception-free games, but it seemed like a mirage. His judgment and field vision haven't suddenly taken a quantum leap in the twilight of his career. Sure enough, Smith has thrown an interception in each of his last three games. Reversion to the mean is a powerful thing.

The other part is the talent that surrounds Smith on offense. The Jets have a good offensive line. They have multiple talented backs. They have solid tight ends, and they have three good receivers in Garrett Wilson, Adonai Mitchell, and rookie Omar Cooper. The defense is a mess, but the offensive talent is the best group the Jets have had in at least a decade. Geno Smith has benefited from the talent around him. Even so, the Jets have scored more than 25 points in a game just once all year, so for all the somewhat gaudy Geno stats, the bottom line is the Jets haven't exactly lit up the scoreboard.

Third-year running back Braelon Allen has been impressive since Breece Hall exited game three with a leg injury, thrusting Allen into the starting role. In the last five games, Allen has been one of the best running backs in the NFL. Hall is likely to finally be back in the lineup for the Jets on Sunday, but I'm hoping, probably in vain, that the Jets have seen that Allen is the better back.

Regardless of which back gets the bulk of the work, the Chiefs have been one of the best teams in the NFL in stopping the run, so if the Jets are to have any chance in this game, I would guess Geno Smith and the Jets receivers will need to have a big game. Omar Cooper might be the guy to look out for, as well as fellow rookie tight end Kenyon Sadiq. If Garrett Wilson doesn't play, those two are the most talented weapons the Jets have in the passing game.

The Jets' 11th ranking in total defense is a sham. The Jets defense stinks. They have one good pass rusher, rookie David Bailey. The interior defensive line is a shambles with both David Onyemata and T'Vondre Sweat out injured. The linebackers are slow and mostly ineffective. The same can be said about the cornerbacks. Safety Dane Belton has been a revelation in his first year with the Jets after spending his early career with the Giants. He's been really good, especially against the run. The other safety, Minkah Fitzpatrick, is in steep decline, living on reputation after a stellar career.

This is an untalented Jets defense that had the good fortune to face some bad offenses, some backup quarterbacks and some teams with injury problems early in the year. Lately, the results have deteriorated badly. The Jets have allowed 26+ points on defense in each of the last three games. I expect that to be more of what we can expect from the Jets defense going forward.

This shouldn't be a challenging game for the Kansas City offense. I expect Kenneth Walker to run wild against a porous Jets run defense, and I expect Patrick Mahomes to pick apart a mediocre Jets secondary. If the Jets have any chance for an unlikely upset on Sunday, it will probably have to be in a shootout. I just don't see the Jets defense shutting down Mahomes, Walker, Rice and company.

The Jets have one chance against the Chiefs: a Chiefs letdown. It isn't always easy getting up for the dregs of the league. Sometimes you look past a lesser opponent. If the Chiefs come out flat, maybe the Jets can steal a win if the Jets offense plays up to its talent level. However, Kansas City is a very difficult venue for Chiefs opponents. Even if the Chiefs come out flat, it's likely the Jets' bad coaching will blow the opportunity for an upset. The gap between future Hall of Fame coach Andy Reid and future out-of-work coach Aaron Glenn is enormous. It will be difficult for the Jets to overcome that.

If the Chiefs don't come out flat, this game shouldn't be close. I am terrible at score predictions, but let's go with Chiefs 27, Jets 16.

#3013. Inside the card-grading empire shaping the price of Pokémon cards

Collectors may have more grading options than ever, but many still turn to Professional Sports Authenticator, or PSA — even as the industry leader works through a backlog of millions of cards.

Demand for grading — an act of verifying a card’s authenticity and condition — has grown as collectors increasingly treat trading and vintage sports cards as alternative investments.

PSA reported a backlog of 7.2 million items on Oct. 20, down from 12.4 million in July. The company suspended cheaper submission services in June to ease the backlog before reopening its Value service on Oct. 29.

Even so, some sellers would rather wait for a PSA label than send their cards to a competitor, dealers told CNBC.

“It really all comes down to secondary market values,” said Geoff Wilson, founder and CEO of CardsHQ, which operates one of the world's largest sports card shops.

PSA-graded cards typically command higher prices than equivalent grades from competitors, Wilson said, giving collectors and investors an incentive to choose PSA in hopes of a stronger return.

The financial difference can be substantial.

A 1999 Base Set Charizard graded PSA 9 sold for about $3,440 on eBay on Sept. 29, compared with roughly $1,890 for a CGC 9 copy sold the same day, according to CNBC's review of PriceCharting records.

That pricing gap reinforces PSA's lead and gives its decisions about fees and submission services considerable influence over the resale market.

PSA had not responded to CNBC’s detailed questions at the time of publication. In published updates, the company has attributed service restrictions and pricing changes to demand exceeding capacity, and outlined investments intended to reduce the backlog.

Grading companies verify a card’s authenticity, assess its condition and seal it in a protective plastic case, known as a slab. PSA uses a 10-point scale, with its highest grade, Gem Mint 10, reserved for cards it describes as "virtually perfect."

Like a credit rating in bond markets, a card grade gives buyers and sellers a common benchmark for assessing condition.

That can reduce friction in transactions by giving both sides a reference point based on past sales of the same card at the same grade.

That capability has helped open the trading-card market to more global participation and made cross-border transactions easier, according to an unnamed spokesperson for Singapore-based submission dealer Oxley Grading.

The spokesperson attributed PSA’s position as the industry’s “gold standard” to its early start, brand recognition and extensive history of completed sales.

“They've just built up such a strong branding and a lot of sales records,” the spokesperson said.

They estimated that roughly 90% of the graded cards offered to Oxley Grading for purchase carry PSA’s label.

The dominance creates a self-reinforcing cycle: buyers seek out PSA-graded cards, giving sellers an incentive to use the company. The abundance of PSA slabs in shops and online marketplaces further reinforces the brand's visibility and demand.

For a seller choosing a grader, that established resale demand can outweigh a competitor’s lower fees or faster turnaround times.

PSA sits within a larger collectibles business.

Its parent company, Collectors, also owns rival grading brands Beckett and SGC, pricing-data platform Card Ladder, the PSA Vault and financial services. The vault links storage with selling through external marketplaces, including eBay.

Together, those businesses span much of a card’s commercial journey — grading, pricing, storage, sales and even financing against collectibles.

Collectors' three card-grading brands accounted for roughly 71% of August grading volume among the major firms tracked by industry analyst GemRate. PSA alone represented about 65%, while the next-largest player, CGC, represented about 28%.

Collectors' consolidation has also drawn legal scrutiny.

A proposed class action filed in April challenges Collectors’ acquisitions of SGC and Beckett, alleging that the deals unlawfully reduced competition in card grading. The allegations have not been proven in court.

Collectors has defended the Beckett acquisition as a way to strengthen a trusted brand while preserving collectors’ ability to choose the grading service that best suits their items.

“We believe that strong, well-supported brands help grow access to the hobby,” Collectors CEO Nat Turner said in the company's 2025 acquisition announcement.

For sellers who prioritize PSA's resale premium, changes to its fees and submission services can alter the economics of grading a card.

PSA raised fees across several existing tiers in February, including Value Bulk from $21.99 to $24.99 and Value from $27.99 to $32.99. It also lengthened estimated turnaround times for some services, saying the changes would help balance submission demand with grading capacity.

The suspension of cheaper tiers in June further narrowed affordable options, while higher costs have made customers more selective about which cards they submit, according to Oxley Grading's spokesperson.

Meanwhile, Wilson said the restrictions have particularly affected collectors and dealers who buy cards ungraded, send them to PSA and then resell them at a higher price.

For cards worth a few hundred dollars or less, that strategy has become harder to justify, he said.

“You're seeing more raw cards bought and sold at card shows,” Wilson added, using the industry term for ungraded cards.

PSA has acknowledged that customers are waiting too long.

In an Oct. 28 update, the company said submissions in April and May were 2.5 times the volume received in the same months a year earlier. It also announced shorter turnaround estimates for Standard and Priority services, while saying new Value submissions would be processed behind existing orders.

“We have added an incredible amount of capacity, but we still have work to do,” Ryan Hoge, president of PSA and grading at Collectors, said in the statement.

For Wilson, those investments in capacity are part of PSA's appeal.

“They've made the collector experience better by doing that,” he said.

The leading graders' dominance may leave collectors with little room to push back.

Collectors' preference for PSA's resale value can make switching graders less attractive, the Oxley Grading's spokesperson said, adding, "PSA's lead comes from resale demand and liquidity, and other labels are still building that."

Overseas customers can face fewer practical grading options as many major graders are based in the U.S., Amber Chuah, operations manager of trading-card platform TCG Marketplace, told CNBC.

The complaints she hears most often involve long waits and disappointment when cards receive lower grades than customers expected.

Challenging a grade can require another round of shipping and waiting, discouraging some collectors from disputing the result, she added.

"If you're really keen on selling, that's a lot of time."

PSA did not respond to CNBC's questions about the sources' claims.

PSA’s terms allow customers to request a paid review of disputed grades, with customers responsible for grading and applicable shipping fees. The company also offers defect notes for eligible submissions to provide more detail on how a grade was determined.

CardsHQ's Wilson said some variation between assessments is an inherent challenge in a process that involves human judgment.

Wilson pushes back against the suggestion that PSA’s position leaves customers without alternatives.

“First of all, there are alternatives,” he said, pointing to CGC, Beckett and smaller grading firms such as TAG.

CGC’s grading volume in August was more than double its level a year earlier, according to GemRate, showing that rival graders are attracting business. Beckett also recorded strong growth, although it is owned by Collectors.

Oxley Grading's spokesperson said there may be room for lower-cost services aimed at collectors who care more about a card’s artwork, sentimental value or preservation than maximizing its resale value.

Competing for sellers focused on the strongest financial return remains more difficult.

Building comparable resale acceptance would require substantial investment, with no guarantee that buyers would ultimately value a newcomer's label as highly as PSA's, the spokesperson said.

For Wilson, broader acceptance in the resale market is key to shifting that balance.

“If the market as a whole starts to respect the value of the other grading companies' cards more, then collectors will increasingly submit to those companies,” he said.

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#3011. Trump says Stephen Miller to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff

President Donald Trump on Friday announced White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller will be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump revealed the stunning shake-up just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate and scrambling the trajectory of Trump's last two years in office.

Elevating Miller, an architect of Trump's controversial mass-deportation efforts, is bound to raise the hackles of the incoming class of congressional Democrats, who have already vowed to investigate the government's immigration policies.

"It has been the privilege of my lifetime to have served on his team from Day One, and I am honored beyond words to serve as his White House Chief of Staff," Miller said on X later Friday.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our President and defend this magnificent Republic," he said.

Miller will take over starting Monday, Trump said in a Truth Social post, praising his longtime aide as "one of my most trusted advisors."

Wiles, "who has done an outstanding job, will be moving over to the Trump Political Operation," the president wrote.

Asked about the move later Friday afternoon, Trump said, "I wanted to make a change."

"I think Susie's done a very good job, great job. But I just wanted to make it," Trump told reporters as he left the White House for an event in Cape Canaveral, Florida.

"Stephen's been great," Trump said, adding, "Sometimes in life you have to make moves."

Wiles resigned Thursday night, a senior White House source told MS NOW. Wiles told Trump, who was said to be angry with her following the GOP's huge election losses, "let me make this easy on you — I am going to resign," MS NOW reported.

"Today is my last day as White House Chief of Staff," Wiles said in an X post Friday afternoon. "Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime."

"While my role may be changing, my commitment to the President and the mission never will," she wrote.

Senior administration officials were quick to congratulate Miller.

"Patriots in control," read an X post from Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, whose job security has come into question after some Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, blamed the midterm results on the Iran war.

Vance also praised Miller on X, saying he "will do great things."

The official X account for Trump's political operation celebrated the announcement by posting a black-and-white photo of Miller under the caption, "It's Miller time."

Democrats and other critics, meanwhile, filed in to denounce the pick.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who could be elected House speaker next year, called Miller "a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands."

"His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today," Jeffries wrote on X.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., wrote that Trump "learned nothing from his historic defeat."

"Stephen Miller is the architect of the most barbaric, inhumane policies of this administration. Trump couldn't have made a worse choice. Our Democratic majorities will fight him every step of the way," Schumer wrote.

Others took Miller's promotion as a sign that Trump, who seemed to express some willingness to negotiate with a blue Congress, will instead remain combative.

"For those who hoped the @POTUS might pivot to work with the incoming Congress, the appointment of Miller is a bucket of ICE cold water," wrote David Axelrod, a former senior advisor to President Barack Obama.

The White House reshuffle follows multiple resignations of senior staff in the Department of Justice since Election Day. They include Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division, and Jason Reding Quiñones, who had been in charge of conspiracy investigations related to Trump's foes.

Wiles oversaw Trump's winning 2024 presidential campaign and has wielded major influence in his second administration, while largely staying out of the spotlight. She drew focus in late 2025, when Vanity Fair reported that Wiles admitted that Trump had pursued "retribution" as president and that he had an "alcoholic's personality."

Trump, and many other top administration officials, defended Wiles at the time, underscoring her status in the White House.

While Wiles is not fully leaving Trump's orbit, her resignation follows a pattern of other top female officials who have left or been fired from Trump's second administration, and then replaced by men.

#3010. Why Joe Mazzulla hugged Neemias Queta instead of yelling at him in win over Hornets

CHARLOTTE — Early in the third quarter of the Celtics' 119-80 win over the Charlotte Hornets, Joe Mazzulla called a timeout and walked over to Neemias Queta.

Queta had just gotten tangled up with multiple Hornets players while trying to secure a rebound. And, as is often the case, Mazzulla grew frustrated with his big man's longstanding tendency to fall.

But this time, instead of reaming him out, Mazzulla chose to embrace him.

"I'm just trying to channel my anger into love," Mazzulla said afterward.

"Instead of just yelling at him, I gave him a hug."

It's no secret that Mazzulla is hard on the Portuguese big man, who began his journey with the Celtics on a two-way contract in 2023 and blossomed into the Celtics' productive and reliable starting center last season.

Sometimes, Mazzulla's yelling gets picked up by the TV broadcast. More often than not, his anger is visible from press row.

And, none of that tough love is something that the Celtics head coach denies.

"I'm hard on Neemi," Mazzulla said on Friday night. "I'm hard on our bigs. Those guys, the way they need to play for us, there's a ton of pressure on them. We talk about it all the time — the fives have the toughest job on this team because they got to do a ton on both ends of the floor."

Mazzulla has frequently discussed how difficult the center position is in the team's system, particularly as of late. That's also applied to Mitchell Robinson, who has struggled in his acclimation to the Celtics as he takes on more responsibility in a new system.

Still, Mazzulla wants to make sure that amid his tough coaches style, there's an understanding that all of it is rooted in love. He visits players in the offseason in order to better understand what makes them tick; this past summer, he joined Queta in Portugal, Mitchell Robinson in Nashville, and Luka Garza in Iowa.

In difficult moments, there's a relationship that runs deeper than basketball.

"I appreciate that they allow me to be hard on them, but I also want them to know I appreciate them," he said. "Just trying to do that for them."

Queta is not the only player to get a hug from Joe Mazzulla as of late; Payton Pritchard got an in-game hug from Mazzulla in a win over the Chicago Bulls, after a couple of high-impact plays.

"Joe challenged me," Pritchard said after that win. "I feel like the first four games my defensive activity and my rebounding have been poor… He's challenging me to step up in that area. I think the hug was for me doing that."

On Friday, Pritchard reflected on how Mazzulla has successfully tapped into both sides of the emotional spectrum as a coach.

"Joe, he's a competitor, and he has a lot of emotion, so it brings the best out of us, and it's also cool when you are doing well; he shows the emotion of a loving brother type situation," Pritchard said. "And then we'll have moments where we get into it, and we have disagreements, and it's all right. It's just part of it — we go through love and hate."

Pritchard said his bond with Mazzulla has strengthened over the years; he's been playing for him since he took over as head coach in 2022, and it's come with plenty of variance. Pritchard has gone through stretches in which he's fallen out of the rotation, won Sixth Man of the Year, and was a part of the 2024 championship team.

"It's a journey," he said. "We've definitely gone through a lot of different situations, a lot of ups and downs, and you just grow your relationship through that, and it becomes stronger and stronger."

Mazuzlla echoed that sentiment last week, after his hug.

"I like pushing him, I like coaching him, and I love him like a brother," Mazzulla said of Pritchard.

For Mazzulla, turning anger into love has been the result of an intentional effort, especially when it comes to his relationship with Queta.

"A lot of meditation, a lot of journaling, a lot of just not being so mad at him all the time," Mazzulla said. "But I love him."

Still, Mazzulla's ability to leverage anger hasn't gone away, the Celtics' veterans say.

"It just depends on the day," said Jayson Tatum with a smile. "Some good days, some bad days."

"They're both needed," Pritchard said. "You can't have love without the anger."

#3008. Investors brace for lower Anthropic valuation, calling $1.5 trillion a 'stretch'

Following tech's selloff on Wall Street this week, some investors are lowering their expectations for Anthropic.

The artificial intelligence company had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation in its IPO up until recently, but stakeholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

The change in sentiment comes alongside growing concerns about the outsized spending by AI companies and a sharp post-midterm slump in the market. The Nasdaq plummeted more than 5% on Thursday, its steepest drop in 19 months, reflecting fears that Democrats' newfound power in Washington, D.C., and a nationwide rebuke of AI will lead to a slowdown in the buildout of data centers.

Bearishness on the AI trade was on the rise before the election. The Nasdaq suffered its worst month in October since March 2025, led by declines in chipmakers and neoclouds.

Anthropic is set to list on the Nasdaq, under the ticker ANT, and is gearing up for an expected debut in a matter of weeks. Last month, Anthropic was reportedly seeking a valuation as high as $2 trillion. As of May, the company was privately valued at $965 billion.

With the latest market turmoil, one investor said $1 trillion to $1.25 trillion is a more likely valuation target, although that could change with market conditions. The investors asked not to be named because they're not authorized to speak about the matter.

Gil Luria, an analyst at D.A. Davidson, told CNBC that, "Investors will expect the IPO to be priced at least at the low end of previous expectations."

"The market really needs this IPO to go well and the bankers on the deal surely understand that," Luria said. "The best way to set Anthropic up for success is to price the deal well."

The company is expected to kick off its roadshow next week.

#3007. World’s No. 1 restaurant crowned as contest comes under increasing scrutiny

Asador Etxebarri in Atxondo, Spain, is the world's top restaurant.

That's according to The World's 50 Best Restaurants, which announced results on Wednesday evening in Lima, Peru. Asador Etxebarri, helmed by chef Bittor Arguinzoniz, sits in Basque Country and features grilled items like a charred beef chop, fresh mozzarella from the chef's buffalo, or house-made chorizo.

Chef Gaggan Anand's Gaggan in Bangkok, which combines progressive Indian cuisine with French, Thai and Japanese influences, was No. 2, and Alchemist in Copenhagen, from head chef Rasmus Munk, took the third spot.

India returned to the top 50 for the first time since 2004, with Masque at No. 26 and NAAR at No. 49. Mainland China made the list for the first time since 2021, with Meet the Bund in Shanghai at No. 21 and Ru Yuan in Hangzhou at No. 36.

Chef-restaurateur Ferran Adrià, who achieved worldwide renown with his avant-garde techniques at his now-closed restaurant El Bulli, received the SevenRooms Icon Award. Adria continues to support culinary research and education through the nonprofit El Bulli Foundation.

The 50, as the umbrella organization is called, creates lists of restaurants, bars, hotels and vineyards that it ranks as top in the world or region. It draws on industry networks to help rank establishments.

For years, The 50 has been seen as a kingmaker and an opportunity for restaurants and bars worldwide. Being "on the list," many chefs and other restaurant-industry insiders have said, makes a huge difference for reservations and revenues.

But there's been increased scrutiny of the contest as well. Chef Ana Roš of Hiša Franko in Slovenia, which was ranked 60th this year, posted on Instagram that she wouldn't be attending the Lima ceremony, saying she would rather invest resources "in our guests, our team, our producers and the daily work of making the restaurant better."

While stressing that she had enjoyed a "vibrant community" at the 50 Best and made many friends from it, she said "over time, however, success within the ranking has come to depend increasingly on visibility within the system itself," and that promotion of one's restaurant risked becoming as important as the work happening inside it.

She appears not to have been alone. Many of the world's top chefs consider awards and lists to be a tricky balance with their work at their establishment.

"Guides and lists now shape the industry they claim to assess," Ivan Brehm, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Nouri in Singapore, told CNBC. "Their judgments influence where diners spend money, where investment flows, which chefs get attention and what younger cooks learn to aspire to."

He added of the 50 that "none of this denies the brilliance of the restaurants on the list or the opportunities it has created for all of us. The question is what it sidelines and what it incentivizes."

Roš' Instagram post had more than 10,400 likes as of Friday, including from top chefs worldwide.

"The reaction and the reach were unexpected" from the post, Roš told CNBC in an email. "I did not write a post to provoke, but more as an eye-opener. And apparently a lot of people have the same perception as I do."

The 50 defended its list and role within the industry.

"The 50 does not claim to have the capabilities to police establishments on their budgets or surrounding activities," a spokesperson told CNBC. "The 50's role is to provide a snapshot of expert opinions on the best hospitality experiences in a given region or category. It does not act as a regulatory body and it applies the same voting rules and principles consistently across all countries and regions."

The time and effort put into the contest can be a dilemma, too.

"We didn't go to Lima this year. It's a huge amount of time in the air for a very short amount of time on the ground and, like Ana, I felt my time was better spent being present in our restaurant with our team and our guests," said Dave Pynt, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Burnt Ends in Singapore, who won the 50 Best Chef's Choice Award in 2017.

However, he added that "when the awards are closer and easier to get to, I love going. They're fun, you learn a lot, and you get to catch up with mates and people in the industry you don't get to see enough."

#3005. Player Grades: Lakers vs. Blazers

For so long in the LeBron James-Anthony Davis era, the Lakers were searching for 3-and-D wings to put around their stars.

One of the best parts of this current Lakers team is the glut of wings. Not only does it allow them to have multiple of them on the floor throughout the game, but it also affords them options depending on who is playing well.

On Friday, for example, Matisse Thybulle, who has been a consistent contributor this season, struggled. The Lakers were able to turn to Jake LaRavia, then Ziaire Williams and even debuted Adou Thiero as well. It's such a luxury that this team hasn't had for years, allowing them to pick and choose the best groupings each night.

So, let's dive into the win. As always, grades are based on expectations for each player. A "B" grade represents the average performance for that player.

24 minutes, 2 points, 2 rebounds, 2 fouls, 3 steals, 2 turnovers, 0-1 FG, -8

As mentioned, this wasn't a great game for Thybulle. He had a couple of sloppy closeouts early and committed some frustrating turnovers.

24 minutes, 7 points, 8 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal, 6 fouls, 1 turnover, 2-5 FG, 2-3 3PT, 1-4 FT, -16

Something about playing the Blazers messes with LaRavia's brain or something, because his two worst games of the year have come against them. Fortunately in this outing, he was able to mix in plenty of good moments to still be a positive contributor.

37 minutes, 16 points, 12 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal, 5 fouls, 1 turnover, 7-10 FG, 2-3 FT, +8

Another quietly impressive game from Kessler, who held his own against Donovan Clingan throughout the night. His funniest basket of the night came toward the end when the Blazers forgot about him in front of the rim on an inbound.

38 minutes, 25 points, 3 rebounds, 2 assists, 4 steals, 4 fouls, 2 turnovers, 8-22 FG, 1-8 3PT, 8-8 FT, -6

Austin was at the center of the Lakers' poor start, going 4-15 from the field in the first three quarters. But when he was needed, Reaves responded in the fourth, scoring 15 points on 4-7 shooting, including a huge steal and dunk late.

38 minutes, 53 points, 5 rebounds, 5 assists, 1 steal, 3 fouls, 3 turnovers, 17-33 FG, 5-15 3PT, 14-18 FT, +16

You know what kind of run Luka is on right now? The type where, from the moment he let go of that shot from the opposite free throw line, every Lakers fan immediately thought, "That looks good."

20 minutes, 2 points, 2 steals, 2 fouls, 1 turnover, 1-3 FG, 0-2 3PT, +19

Grimes still looks a bit of a mess on offense. It screams of overthinking and not finding a rhythm. Even as he's struggled, though, he's still been a positive on the defensive end.

But they could really use him finding a groove on offense.

20 minutes, 7 points, 3 rebounds, 1 assist, 1 steal, 1 block, 2-6 FG, 1-3 3PT, 2-2 FT, +19

We got a look at the Mamu-Kessler front court in the first half tonight, something the team probably hasn't been able to do as much as they wished. Mamu has had to be used as the backup five, not leaving many minutes for a jumbo-sized frontcourt.

19 minutes, 8 points, 3 rebounds, 1 assist, 2 steals, 1 foul, 1 turnover, 3-5 FG, 2-4 3PT, +8

Having a player like Ziaire available to come off the bench is such a luxury. His tip of the pass late in the fourth that Austin was then able to steal was a crucial play to help seal the win.

10 minutes, 1 assist, 5 fouls, 0-4 FG, 0-1 3PT, -10

I feel like Sexton has been afforded an appropriate amount of time for not getting a preseason. This is just really, really bad now. If you wanted to find a place for minutes for Cameron Carr, Sexton can't be far away from being taken out of the rotation.

While he only played six minutes, Thiero was fantastic. The Lakers were using him as a screener and lob threat and, unironically, it's a great use of him. He got a lob and found Ziaire for a dunk of his own in the first half.

His energy and activity level is always so high that he just makes an impact.

I'll give Redick credit for what I mentioned above in his use of Thiero, who replaced Jarred Vanderbilt in the rotation. The team, realistically, can't survive having two non-shooting wings on the floor and investing in Thiero is the right call.

I'll knock him for that second challenge, though. But overall, it was a solid night from him.

Friday's inactives: Cameron Carr, Bronny James, Arthur Kaluma, Chris Mañon

#3004. Democrat shaping AI policy bought chip stocks despite backing congressional trading ban

Rep. Josh Gottheimer, a Democrat helping write his party's artificial intelligence agenda, has disclosed new purchases of AI-linked stocks — just months after voting to ban lawmakers and their families from buying individual shares.

The New Jersey Democrat disclosed an Oct. 12 purchase of between $1,001 and $15,000 of Benchmark Electronics stock, according to a filing released this week. His jointly owned Morgan Stanley account also bought up to $15,000 worth of Applied Materials shares on Oct. 26, following three earlier purchases of leading AI chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices.

While there's no evidence of wrongdoing, the trades put Gottheimer's investments in the spotlight as Democrats prepare to take control of Congress in January, gaining new power to regulate and investigate the AI industry.

Gottheimer, a former Microsoft executive, sits on the House Financial Services subcommittee overseeing AI in finance, has introduced legislation on AI regulations and co-chairs a commission developing Democrats' broader AI agenda.

Gottheimer's spokesman, Haraden Bottomley, said the congressman turned over control of his investments to a third party with "full investment discretion" before taking office in 2017.

"Gottheimer is also awaiting approval from Congress of a blind trust. In the meantime, he has taken the extra step of setting up an independent trust," Bottomley said.

Democrats are already targeting the AI industry. Reps. Maxine Waters and Greg Casar introduced a resolution opposing taxpayer bailouts for AI companies, referred in part to Gottheimer's committee. Casar has also called for AI executives to testify under oath, while other Democrats are pushing to restrict data centers or halt new construction altogether.

The purchases also contrast with Gottheimer's longstanding support for restricting congressional stock trading. In July, he was among just 13 Democrats who voted for the Stop Insider Trading Act, which would prohibit lawmakers and their families from purchasing most individual stocks. The bill passed the House but has not become law. Some Democrats opposed it both because it contained a voter-ID provision and because some said it should have gone further in curbing stock purchasing and holding by elected officials.

Four years earlier, Gottheimer backed legislation requiring lawmakers to place investments in blind trusts and pledged to establish one himself.

The AI purchases are just a small slice of Gottheimer's sprawling investment portfolio. The five trades totaled between $5,005 and $75,000, compared with $15.1 million to $59 million in disclosed household assets, including up to $44.5 million in stocks. His household has reported more than 1,100 securities transactions over three years, according to Follow the Filings. Gottheimer reports household assets jointly with his wife, Marla Tusk, who is general counsel at a political consulting firm.

Advanced Micro Devices competes with Nvidia in AI chips, while Applied Materials supplies semiconductor manufacturing equipment. It was also Benchmark's largest disclosed customer in 2025, accounting for 14% of revenue, according to a securities filing.

Benchmark, meanwhile, generated 30% of its third-quarter revenue from semiconductor equipment and another 16% from advanced computing and communications, according to its Oct. 28 earnings report. Gottheimer also disclosed an Applied Materials purchase in August and a sale in September.

The purchases came shortly before a sharp selloff in AI stocks. The Nasdaq Composite Index plunged 5.5% Thursday, as Nvidia dropped more than 9% and AMD roughly 17%. The index includes both companies.

Benchmark fell nearly 17% over Wednesday and Thursday, closing at $59.34 — roughly 25% below its closing price on Gottheimer's October purchase date — before recovering somewhat Friday.

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#3002. Data center double-down: Hassett says blue wave won't stop AI build-out

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett said Friday he expects data center growth to continue as projected, despite the blue-wave election that saw voters overwhelmingly favor limiting the tech infrastructure buildout.

Hassett, on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street," acknowledged the dozens of measures on states' midterm ballots seeking to constrain data centers, a crucial part of President Donald Trump's push to win the global artificial intelligence race.

In at least 46 of the roughly 50 such initiatives on ballots in 2026, majorities voted to restrict development of the facilities, according to Ballotpedia's tally.

"We respect the localities that decide that's what they want to do," Hassett said. "But America is a big place, and right now, the data centers scheduled over the next couple of years aren't affected by these ballot initiatives."

"And the stuff after that, you know, there's plenty of room in the U.S. for data center growth to continue, as it was projected ahead of the election," he said.

Hassett said he expects data centers will continue to expand in states with more welcoming governors, and he suggested that even hostile states will eventually come around.

"That's exactly what's going to happen," he said. "That, or what'll happen will be that the benefits of the data centers will be so obvious that people decide to change their minds about the way they regulate them."

Hassett also blamed Thursday's tech-driven market plunge on "uncertainty about what the political change is going to mean for data centers going forward."

"We've now got a ... new majority that maybe is going to go in there and muddle around in there, and I think that markets are anxious about that," he said.

"But I think that the economic strength that we see everywhere else is so strong, and of course, in the end, you could take something that's growing as fast as the data centers and make it grow a little bit slower, and you still have a blockbuster economy."

"And so, I think we're still very, very optimistic about what's going to happen going forward," he said.

The CNBC Voter Poll found 56% of midterm voters identified data centers as either a crucial or major issue — and of those who considered it crucial, 62% favored the generic Democratic candidate.

Trump has strongly embraced AI and data centers and has even tried to reverse their seeming unpopularity by rebranding the tech as "super intelligence," or SI.

In a Truth Social post Friday, Trump made clear that despite the midterm losses, his support for the nascent industry has not faltered.

"As President, I am 100% in favor of Super Intelligence (SI), and will work with the many States in the U.S. that want to become RICH by having them," Trump wrote. "They are GOLD, and only foolish leadership will say 'NO' to the riches they bring!"

#3000. Microsoft plans to use fewer neoclouds as AI buildout shows signs of strain

Microsoft is shrinking its roster of neocloud partners as it focuses on work with a few key suppliers and builds out its own data centers, according to people familiar with the matter.

The software giant's plans pose another potential challenge for a corner of the artificial intelligence market that's showing signs of strain due to rising borrowing costs, hefty debt loads and concerns about whether the buildout will slow down.

Microsoft has forged relationships in recent years with a number of neoclouds, including CoreWeave, Nebius, Chronoscale, IREN, Lambda and Nscale, as well as with Oracle, which is pouring money into building out AI infrastructure.

Microsoft is now in the evaluation stage and hasn't decided which neoclouds it will stop using, said two sources, who asked not to be named because the talks are confidential. One of the people said Microsoft wants to prioritize its own data center footprint. Bloomberg reported on the plans earlier. Two people said that Microsoft opted not to renew a deal with Oracle.

A Microsoft spokesperson said in an emailed statement that its infrastructure strategy is "built on flexibility and choice."

"We combine Microsoft-owned datacenters, leased facilities and strategic partnerships with third-party providers to deliver the capacity our customers need, where and when they need it," the spokesperson said. "We regularly review these investments against customer demand. That includes tracking delivery and adjusting plans as circumstances change."

The neoclouds have been a turbulent trade on Wall Street in recent weeks as rising interest rates added to fears that many companies have become overly reliant on debt in rushing to build capacity. Shares of CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all down by roughly half over the past month even after rallying on Friday.

A recent industry review counted 323 neoclouds, and experts have predicted that many of them won't survive.

In its earnings report on Thursday, IREN said it handed over data center capacity to Microsoft in August, but Microsoft requested a networking equipment upgrade that will result in the infrastructure coming online in January or February instead of this year.

Microsoft finance chief Amy Hood said on the software company's fiscal first-quarter earnings call in October that some cloud contracts involving graphics processing units were delayed until the first half of 2027, affecting Azure cloud growth. Microsoft reported $45.2 billion worth of capital expenditures and finance leases during the latest quarter, and said capex spending will accelerate in the second half of the fiscal year.

CoreWeave and Nebius are both scheduled to report quarterly results next week. IREN shares popped 16% on Friday after the company said AI revenue more than doubled from the prior quarter to $200 million. IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told CNBC that there's a "disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level."

However, he acknowledged the challenge that market conditions present.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the earnings call.

Microsoft has already slashed its work with U.K.-based neocloud Nscale, walking away from a site that it had intended to use in England. Darren Hardman, Microsoft's CEO for the U.K and Ireland, told CNBC in October that the decision had to do with Nscale's ability to get access to electricity.

The Financial Times reported this week that Microsoft decided against renewing a contract with Nscale in Texas. Nscale filed to go public in mid-September but hasn't updated its prospectus since then. The company said in its IPO filing that it expects Microsoft to be a "significant customer in future periods."

Microsoft's big turn to neoclouds came after the launch of ChatGPT in 2022, which led to a surge in demand for GPUs. In looking elsewhere, Microsoft signed up CoreWeave, which was a privately held startup at the time with roots in cryptocurrency mining. When CoreWeave went public last year, it said 62% of its 2024 revenue came from Microsoft.

At the time of ChatGPT's release, OpenAI only used Azure. Now the company's cloud suppliers also include Amazon, Cerebras, CoreWeave, Google and Oracle.

Microsoft's spokesperson said that its approach to reviewing neocloud investments is "a normal and responsible way to manage large, complex infrastructure projects," and that "the pace of our new agreements reflects what we need today."

—CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed to this report.

#2998. Why a wave of bomb threats is proving so difficult for Europe's aviation authorities to contain

A spate of bomb threats against passenger flights has forced diversions, emergency landings and military escorts across European airspace this week, stoking fear among passengers and heaping pressure on airlines.

Authorities have described the more than two dozen threats as malicious hoaxes, and no actual attacks have been carried out. But the incidents have highlighted the huge impact that even false claims can have on a tightly scheduled aviation network.

"This is really quite exceptional, I've never seen this before in my whole career," Wilfried Covent, head of security firm Covent Consulting with a specialization in aviation, told CNBC by phone.

"We've had bomb threats in flights and towards airports, but usually maybe a single copycat, one or two max. What we see now is a systematic attack on airlines and airports."

An email sent to European broadcasters on Friday claimed to be from the leader of the group behind the plane hoaxes and also said bombs had been placed in their buildings, prompting evacuations. No bombs were found at the sites.

"What happened this week is just a small part of what awaits Europe in the near future," the message said, according to Reuters.

The news agency said it could not verify the sender's identity, where the emails originated or whether they were linked to the threats against flights.

Covent said the "criminals" — who many security analysts say are likely to be state-backed actors working in coordination to disrupt public life for Europeans, but have not been identified — were well aware that the "weakest point of aviation is targeting it in-flight."

"With a threat on an airport or a general threat to aircraft, you can assess the situation without fully disrupting operations. When the threat is called on a specific flight number that is in the air, even with a hoax, that triggers international regulations which ensure you don't ignore it — it means a quick landing, evacuation and searches by specialist forces," he told CNBC.

"For the context of an assessment, you cannot say that 20 times before it has been a hoax so this time it is a hoax, the safety of passengers and crew is priority number one. The criminals know that."

Responsibility for dealing with hoax attacks is split, international agencies told CNBC. Assessing security threats and implementing aviation security measures primarily falls to national authorities, working in coordination with airport operators, airlines and law enforcement.

Air traffic control will also be involved in finding aircraft with an appropriate place to land, while the European Union Aviation Safety Agency, or EASA and U.K.'s Civil Aviation Authority more broadly set aviation safety rules and set how airlines prepare their crews to respond to security threats. Within the European Union, the European Commission is involved in aviation security policy and coordination between member states.

The operational decisions also depend on where the threat arises. Joji Waites, director of flight safety, policy and regulation at the British Air Line Pilots Association, said airline assessors grade a threat as red, amber or green, but that an amber verdict would never be given if the plane is airborne.

If assessors judge a threat credible, the crew is informed and the captain takes command of decisions for the aircraft, guided by company procedures. On the ground, that may mean an evacuation, while in the air the priority is to land as soon and as safely as possible.

As well as the fear and disruption to specific flights, each diversion can then affect the next flight scheduled to use that aircraft, the crew's working hours and passengers with connections.

The network effect can be significant. Philip Baum, a visiting professor of aviation security at Coventry University, told AP that a single diversion could cost an airline as much as $250,000, including replacement crews, missed connections and disruption to the rest of the day's schedule.

"Diversions necessitated by these bomb threats are a disturbance to a system designed for predictability and efficiency, and lead to increased workload for pilots, Air Traffic Controllers and airports," an EASA spokesperson said in emailed comments.

"As these staff have all have been trained to cope with such unexpected developments, the immediate safety threat from individual hoax threats is low. However, the higher number of diversions places an additional strain on the system."

National governments will already be looking at their aviation authority regulations and frameworks and there will be discussions at the European and global level, John Edwards, senior consultant at Gates Aviation in London, said by phone.

"Bomb threats to aviation have been going on for decades, what they'll need to be looking at is what's different. Why have there been so many, why have so many been considered sufficiently credible to warrant the kind of action that it's warranted?"

"There's a range of questions they should be able to work through fairly quickly to establish a broad-brush level of credibility. If there is some characteristic that is new, every time there is an event of this kind, it triggers a big collaboration to ensure recurrence is prevented going forward."

"In theory, he said, "the vast majority should be able to assessed and considered non-credible based on routine risk assessment processes and well established procedures."

However, Eric Schouten, a former intelligence liaison officer at the Dutch General Intelligence and Security Service and CEO of security consultancy Dyami, said that changes to existing procedural regulations would take some time and require balancing the views and responsibilities of countries, airports, safety authorities and the European Union, with geopolitics also playing a role.

"There is a high likelihood even more flights are going to be canceled or diverted. It's possible that next to be targeted could be railways and highways," he said.

"But they will figure it out. It's not the first time airlines have had to deal with hoax calls because there are always copycats."

"Whatever measures they come out with to address it, they won't share publicly" for risk of alerting the bad actors, he added.

Wilfried Covent said that while there would not be any quick changes to assessment protocol, "if this is really going on for ever and ever something will need to change or you'd have to stop flying completely."

"Based on what we've seen in the past with other kinds of threats, time goes on and the type of attack may change and move on," he continued, pointing to the wave of cyberattacks that has recently hit supermarket fridges and blood banks.

#2996. Utah Jazz vs Dallas Mavericks: Recap and Final Score

Utah's losing streak extended to seven games after losing to the Dallas Mavericks, 112-100.

The Jazz started this game pretty well overall and showed impressive defense on second-year phenom Cooper Flagg. Ace Bailey deserves a lot of the credit for keeping Flagg to 21 points. Utah also did a good job on Kyrie Irving, holding him to just 35% from the field. Keyonte George has shown solid defense this season, and his defense showed up tonight in his moments guarding Irving and others. It's a great sign for the Jazz's future that he's developing that way. Combine that with Ace Bailey's defense and the emerging play of Darryn Peterson, and the Jazz have something brewing. It's just hard to see when you're in the midst of a prolonged losing streak.

The second half was different for Utah. In the third quarter, the Mavericks overcame the Jazz starters and forced substitutions. That's when Isaiah Collier, Svi Mykhailiuk, and Brice Sensabaugh got minutes again. This bench unit has been bad all season and keeps getting minutes, even after Isaiah Collier's contract wasn't re-upped for next season. Will Hardy is in a tough place with Jaren Jackson Jr. out, and you don't want to run a 7-man lineup all season, but something has to be done to stop these Collier/Sensabaugh/Mykhailiuk minutes. It's costing the Jazz wins night after night.

That said, Hardy deserves credit for some of his adjustments. He's staggered lineups to keep it from being a completely all-bench unit. Tonight it was with Lauri Markkanen in that lineup, but it didn't work. The issue with Markkanen is that you can't just give him the ball and expect him to go get a bucket, he's a purely off-ball player. What should theoretically work is having Keyonte George and Darryn Peterson stagger minutes to make sure one of them is on the floor at all times. Darryn Peterson might not have been quite ready for that to start the season, although I think that's debatable, but after tonight, he's looking more and more ready for a bigger role.

The good in this game was the duo of Keyonte George and Darryn Peterson. Peterson had one of his best statistical performances, shooting 50/42.9/100 with 21 points, 5 assists, and 2 rebounds. Peterson looked more comfortable in the fourth quarter, and you can only hope that it's the beginning of him taking a leap.

Keyonte George also continues a mostly awesome season. He had 16 points, 6 rebounds, and 9 assists in this one. George's production is great, he just needs to cut down on turnovers. If he can keep this production and cut down on the bad turnovers, he's looking at becoming one of the best guards in the league.

Josh² has been a great addition to the team. Josh Green and Josh Okogie have brought great two-way play for the Jazz and are playing well off of the Jazz's young guards. There's a formula here that should work for the Jazz.

The downside is the bench unit, specifically Svi Mykhailiuk, Isaiah Collier, and Brice Sensabaugh. It hasn't worked all season and has cost the Jazz multiple wins. What's even more frustrating is how quickly Will Hardy has been to pull Peterson after a mistake. The issue is that the players he replaces them with aren't playing well or mistake-free themselves. Collier had three turnovers in this one, and a few came from travel calls. That bench unit also gave up the biggest lead and pretty much ended the Jazz's chance at a win. Hardy is in a tough spot missing Jaren Jackson Jr. with a very young team, so there's almost no room for error. If there is one thing that Utah could really use, though, is fewer minutes from those three. If they can give Josh² more minutes, that should help. But even with that, it's going to be tough to get wins.

Finally, Utah is ready for Ace Bailey's threes to start dropping. He's in a big slump right now, and when he comes out of it, it will be huge for the team. His defensive impact is great, and his rebounding is fantastic. Once his three-point shooting comes around, he'll help the Jazz get more win streaks than losing streaks.

#2995. America's newest small business owners with no employees are off to a hot start in AI era

It takes hundreds of billions of dollars for the tech giants to scale artificial intelligence, but it takes just a few AI tools for the smallest companies in the U.S. economy to get up and running with a cost-competitive business model.

Alex Willen expects the 12 e-commerce brands he operates with no employees to generate between $2.5 million and $3 million this year. Kristin Ginn left Microsoft in March and started trnsfrmAItn, an AI-adoption consultancy. Seven months later, she has roughly $250,000 in business received or contracted through year-end with no employees or regular contractors.

"The biggest misconception is that it's just about individual productivity; it's actually about leveraging AI to perform tasks that previously required a whole team," said Stanford economist Erik Brynjolfsson.

Early research on the impact of AI within the solopreneur business world bears watching.

According to Liya Palagashvili, a labor economist and director of the Labor Policy Project at George Mason University's Mercatus Center, new business formation is rising in direct correlation to applications unlikely to become employer firms.

In industries highly exposed to AI, business applications without early signs of hiring rose 26.8% from the first quarter of 2024 through the first quarter of 2026, from roughly 222,000 to 281,000 per quarter.

Over the same period, applications in those industries with stronger indications of eventually becoming employer businesses fell 6.4%. In construction and wholesale trade, which Palagashvili used as a comparison group, non-employer-type applications declined 0.4%.

"What AI is doing is giving those capabilities directly into the hands of the individual," she said.

Palagashvili cautioned that the findings are preliminary and descriptive and do not establish that AI caused the increase. The Census-based measure identifies applications without indicators associated with near-term payroll or hiring plans; it does not show that those businesses ultimately remained one-person firms or never hired employees.

But Willen shows what the economics can look like.

Anthropic's Claude helps with bookkeeping, customer service, advertising, and inventory across his businesses, while OpenAI image tools perform work that once went to photographers and designers.

Without AI, Willen said, "I'd probably need one other full-time person, plus I'd need to use contractors for photography, design, bookkeeping, and ad management."

A traditional product photography shoot could cost $2,000 to $5,000, and a designer $300 to $1,000 for new listing images, he said.

When AI directly eliminates one of those expenses, Willen said, "that money just goes into my pocket."

AI has also allowed him to pursue revenue channels he would not have previously considered. After acquiring Lone Trail, a hunting-gear brand selling binocular harnesses, packs, and other outdoor accessories, Willen said its Shopify site accounted for roughly 1% of sales. Rather than shut down the channel, he used ChatGPT and Claude to rebuild the site and AI to help run Meta advertising. In a recent 30-day period, he said, Shopify accounted for roughly 30% of Lone Trail's revenue.

"That's going to end up being at least six figures of annual revenue that would not exist without AI," Willen said.

Ginn's business shows how quickly a new entrant can reach meaningful revenue without first building an organization around it. She uses AI to help with proposals, pricing, strategy, and other work and is exploring an AI agent to perform part of a labor-intensive client process.

A survey conducted by accounting and invoicing software company FreshBooks identified similar behavior more broadly. Seventy-nine percent of solopreneurs said they had tried AI before hiring someone for a task they could not perform themselves (the same figure was 86% among when including microbusiness owners). AI allows them to "look much bigger than they are," FreshBooks CEO Shaheen Javadizadeh said.

But among existing small businesses, the available data tells another story. Gusto, a payroll and HR software company that serves small businesses, tracked businesses after they adopted AI and found their headcount was about 7% higher after a year than a comparable businesses that knew about AI but had not adopted it. Among firms with fewer than 10 employees, AI adopters grew headcount about 10% more than comparable non-adopters.

Willen's experience also highlights a tradeoff in the new business model: operating with fewer people does not eliminate reliance on larger technology platforms. Roughly 80% of its overall revenue still comes through Amazon, and he uses Meta and Shopify to expand direct sales. Ginn has no staff, but LinkedIn has been one of the biggest drivers of customers to her consultancy, she said.

Brynjolfsson said the proliferation of smaller producers can actually increase the value of the platforms that connect them with the buyers.

"There's this long tail of products, but at the same time, big platforms like Amazon are more powerful than ever because they aggregate all of these and they bring them together," Brynjolfsson said.

But the relationship is not as simple as labor savings flowing directly to Big Tech. Willen said some of the money he saves becomes profit, while Ginn spends less than $60 a month on paid AI tools.

AI can make it economical for more small producers to exist. And as core business production becomes cheaper, small businesses place greater focus on customer acquisition and distribution, Brynjolfsson said.

Brynjolfsson believes that while solopreneurs will thrive, use of AI will at the same time lead to the creation of even larger firms, on average, than exist today.

In a September research report titled "AI's Use of Knowledge in Society," Brynjolfsson and Zoë Hitzig, a researcher at the Anthropic Institute who studies the social and economic impacts of AI, argue that as AI codifies knowledge previously scattered among workers and enables more information to be aggregated and acted upon centrally, firms will grow larger on average, while industry concentration increases and local managerial autonomy declines. The researchers say early evidence is largely consistent with those patterns.

That's bad news for medium-sized companies. Brynjolfsson predicts AI could contribute to a "hollowing out" of midsize firms — very small teams capable of doing more at one end and giant organizations exploiting AI and scale at the other.

"The fat in the middle — the layers of management focused on internal coordination — will likely be significantly reduced," he said.

Adam Branch has experienced a version of that shift from inside two very different technology companies.

Before founding rhino.ai, an enterprise software company that uses AI to modernize legacy systems, Branch built Incentive Technology Group, a traditional technology services company that grew to roughly 500 employees. Under that model, winning more work meant hiring more people, he said.

"With rhino.ai, we're building AI software to do more of the work those teams used to do," said Branch.

Rhino.ai has roughly 50 employees. Branch estimates comparable modernization work can now be performed with three to four times the productivity per employee.

His team estimates labor represented roughly 85% of operating spending at ITG, and technology about 5%. At rhino.ai, Branch puts labor at roughly 40%, while technology, including cloud, software, and AI models, represents about 25%.

AWS sees a similar loosening of the relationship between revenue and headcount among AI-native startups. Its Engines of Growth research, an independent global study of 3,413 startup founders and senior leaders across 20 countries, found those companies grew revenue an average 156% annually versus 65% for startups overall, while more than half generated over $400,000 in revenue per employee. The report focused on companies under five years old that use AI in its most advanced forms.

"Growth used to be tied directly to how many people you could hire," said Ruba Borno, vice president of AWS Specialists and Partners. "Now founders can scale with technology, not just headcount."

Brynjolfsson does not see the result as simply Big Tech winning at everyone else's expense. Entrepreneurs can keep some of the productivity gains, consumers get more choice, and platforms benefit from supplying the technology and connecting more producers with customers, he said.

"The platforms and the people who are creating the tools are winning because they are able to sell them, the solopreneurs are winning because they are able to use them, and the consumers are winning because they are getting new products that they didn't get before," he said. "It's mostly a bigger pie, so it's a win-win-win — a triple win."

What AI may change is the shape of that bigger pie, he said.

"Whenever there's a technology change, a platform change, obviously it benefits the companies that are creating those platforms and technologies," Brynjolfsson said. "But it also benefits new entrants who can start fresh."

#2994. How to watch Commanders vs Rams: TV, radio, streaming, Week 9 injury report

The Washington Commanders are home in front of the fans at Northwest Stadium for the second week in a row, and hosting the Los Angeles Rams in a Week 9 NFC matchup. Both teams need a win to stay alive in their divisions, but the Commanders need it more at 2-4-1.

The Commanders are coming off a come-from-behind 27-10 victory over the Philadelphia Eagles that showed a lot of positive signs. That game gets an asterisks from people because of how bad the Eagles have been over their 6-game losing streak, but they still had a starting defense that couldn't keep the Jayden Daniels and the Commanders from scoring. They will be missing two more of their receivers with Treylon Burks (foot) and Dyami Brown (calf) ruled out.

The Rams are 4-4 and will be getting DE Myles Garrett back for the first time since Week 1. This was a Super Bowl-favorite team, that is now struggling to get above .500 for the season. Matthew Stafford was the NFL MVP last season, but has been throwing more picks this year, putting his team on the wrong side of the turnover differential.

The Commanders a 4.5-point home underdogs, and need to play a complete game to come out of this game with a win. They played good enough last week against a tanking Philly team, but Sean McVay will have his team ready for another return to Washington.

#2988. Lakers’ Ziaire Williams confuses Luka Doncic with Austin Reaves: ‘wrong white person’

Ziaire Williams approached Austin Reaves from behind during his walk-off interview with NBA on Prime, put his hands on his shoulders and yelled, "Fifty ball."

It was Luka Dončić who had scored 53 points in the Lakers' 124-118 win over the Trail Blazers on Friday night at Crypto.com Arena.

"I'm the wrong white person," Reaves, who had 25 points, retorted as he shook his head in disbelief.

Did Williams seriously confuse his two teammates?

"Deadass," Williams said, flashing a smile in the Lakers' locker room.

Dončić had some lighthearted fun with the mixup as well.

"That was very disrespectful to me," Doncic said jokingly.

It was a funny moment following an impressive come-from-behind win for the Lakers, in which they turned a 16-point deficit into a 16-point lead.

Ziaire Williams celebrates after a bucket during the Lakers' 124-118 comeback win over the Trail Blazers on Nov. 6, 2026 in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Mark J. Terrill

Luka Doncic (left) and Austin Reaves look on during the Lakers' win over the Raptors on Nov. 1, 2026, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

#2986. Many workers lose unused FSA money — but could still come out ahead. Here's how

Health flexible spending accounts, or FSAs, are tax-advantaged accounts that let workers set aside pretax money to cover eligible health expenses. However, they're often use it or lose it, with unspent balances forfeited at the end of the plan year.

About 4 in 10 workers with health FSAs forfeited unused money in 2024, according to an October report from the Employee Benefit Research Institute, a nonprofit research organization. Among those FSA account holders, the average forfeiture was $404, down from $436 in 2023. The research examined more than 3.3 million accounts representing $3.9 billion in contributions.

Yet even workers who don't zero out their balance may still benefit from using an FSA, compared with what they would have spent using after-tax dollars to cover the same expenses.

"Even if someone forfeits FSA funds back to their employer, they can still come out ahead — relative to not participating in an FSA — if the amount they forfeited is less than the tax savings they realized," said Jake Spiegel, a senior research associate for EBRI and author of the study.

Nearly three-quarters — 72% — of state and local government workers and 47% of private-sector workers have access to a health FSA in 2026, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That compares with 68% and 40%, respectively, in 2017.

FSAs differ from health savings accounts, or HSAs, which some people use in conjunction with high-deductible health plans. Although both let you set aside pretax money for health care expenses, HSAs let you roll over your balance from year to year, and even invest it.

Roughly a third of FSA users in the EBRI database face a strict use-it-or-lose-it provision, Spiegel said. The rest either are permitted by their employers to carry over an amount — up to $680 in 2026 — into the following year or are offered a grace period of up to 2.5 months after the deadline to incur additional expenses or face forfeiture. However, employers cannot offer both options.

While many plans have a Dec. 31 deadline for spending FSA money, some employers have deadlines falling in other parts of the year, said Rachel Rouleau, chief compliance officer for Health-E Commerce, parent company of online retailers FSA Store and HSA Store.

"Understanding these plan-specific deadlines is essential, so employees should check with their employer or FSA administrator to avoid forfeiting unused funds," Rouleau said.

Additionally, many employers give you extra time — typically three months after the deadline — to submit receipts for qualifying expenses that occurred during the previous plan year.

For 2026, the maximum contribution allowed to health FSAs is $3,400. In 2027, that amount will be $3,500, the IRS announced Thursday.

Part of the reason workers have forfeitures may be that they must decide how much they want to contribute to their FSA before the plan year begins.

"It's difficult to project how much you'll need to spend on health care, especially if you are particularly healthy and don't have a chronic condition with predictable prescription drug costs," Spiegel said.

Contributions to FSAs are withheld from paychecks and generally avoid federal taxes, as well as so-called payroll taxes — known as FICA — that go toward Social Security and Medicare. They also may avoid state and local taxes, depending on where you live.

To determine how much you're saving in taxes by using an FSA, you can add up those tax rates and apply the total to your contribution, said certified financial planner Sean Lovison, founder of Purpose Built Financial Services in Moorestown, New Jersey.

For example, say that your federal, FICA, state and local taxes total 35% and you contributed the maximum allowed — $3,400 for 2026. In this case, the calculation would result in $1,190 in estimated tax savings, which is "how much you're saving on taxes by contributing to your FSA," Lovison said.

The illustration assumes the worker receives no employer contribution to the FSA and the tax rate applies to the entire contribution.

"As long as the number you're forfeiting from your FSA is less than that number, you're going to come out ahead," Lovison said.

Of course, he said, "you don't want to walk away from FSA money, but at the same time, from a mathematical standpoint, you're still coming out ahead."

If you face a forfeiture, you can use it to help inform your decision on how much to put toward your FSA for 2027, if you haven't already made benefit elections for next year.

"Employees should look at their anticipated healthcare expenses over the coming year and consider both their daily health routines and expected annual expenses," Rouleau said. "This includes recurring prescriptions, doctor visits, copays, deductibles, dental and vision care, and other eligible out-of-pocket healthcare expenses for themselves and their eligible dependents."

Before you resign yourself to a forfeiture, keep in mind that there are also many ways to use the money on eligible expenses before the end of the year.

"There are thousands of ways consumers can responsibly spend down their FSA dollars, from everyday healthcare essentials to eligible telehealth services," Rouleau said.

Additionally, "many consumers are surprised to learn that a wide range of over-the-counter medications, menstrual care products, sunscreen, first-aid supplies and certain health-monitoring devices are FSA-eligible," Rouleau said.

That's in addition to doctor and dentist appointments, prescription drugs and other healthcare services such as acupuncture and addiction treatment, according to the IRS.

If you're unsure whether you have money remaining in your FSA or need to spend it by the end of the year, most plans offer an online portal where you can review your balance, submit claims and check deadlines. The FSA administrator's information is typically listed on the back of your FSA card, Rouleau said.
