#2943. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

Maybe the only thing higher than the stakes for Saturday's match-up between No. 2 Georgia and 12th-ranked Mississippi is the level of mutual respect between Bulldogs coach Kirby Smart and Ole Miss counterpart Pete Golding.

[DOWNLOAD: Free WSB-TV News app for alerts as news breaks]

"Pete has done a tremendous job," Smart said.

Since 2023, no two SEC programs have had better records than Georgia (43-7) and Ole Miss (40-9).

This season they also feature highly productive quarterbacks, albeit with contrasting styles.

Georgia (7-1, 5-1 SEC, No. 2 CFP) showcases a clock-eating rushing attack with Nate Frazier, Dwight Phillips Jr. and Bo Walker accounting for 19 touchdowns. Ole Miss (6-2, 4-2 SEC, No. 10 CFP) uses a fast-paced, big-play passing approach, highlighted by quarterback Trinidad Chambliss. The Heisman Trophy candidate has thrown for 2,189 yards and 18 TDs.

"He's a quarterback that makes everybody around him better," Golding said. "He's an elite competitor and tough as nails."

The Rebels have been inconsistent defensively, but showcase transfer linebackers Tajh Boyd, Keaton Thomas and Luke Ferrelli.

Ole Miss is 25-1 at home since 2023 with seven wins against ranked teams, including a 28-10 win over No. 2 Georgia in 2024.

Since 2021, the Bulldogs are an SEC-best 72-8 with four CFP berths and two national championships.

#2941. NFL betting advice: Giants-Eagles picks and props

Welcome to the New York Giants betting angle, Week 9 edition! Each week, I'm giving you my three favorite wagers for that week's New York Giants game. My one rule: I won't ever give you wagers that go beyond -120. No gimmes!

The Giants are coming off their Bye week and heading into what has been a personal house of horrors. Big Blue hasn't won a game in Philadelphia since 2013, but for the first time since 2009, they're favored at the Linc. That tells you a lot about how this season has gone for Philadelphia, losers of six straight.

The first NY-PHI game of this season looks a lot different than it did when the schedule came out. The starting quarterbacks for this game aren't Jaxson Dart and Jalen Hurts. Oh, no. They're not even Jameis Winston and Tanner McKee, who opened the season as the respective backups. No, the starters in Week 9 for these NFC East rivals are J.J. McCarthy and Joe Milton III, each of whom wasn't even on his current team a few short weeks ago. McCarthy has played just five quarters on the Giants and Milton has yet to take a snap for the Eagles. He's also making the first start of his NFL career after appearing in five games across two seasons.

On top of that, there are injury concerns with both starting running backs. Cam Skattebo (hamstring) is listed as doubtful and Saquon Barkley continues to battle through hamstring and back soreness. He'll play, but how much?

What does this all mean? It means I'm flying a little blind this week with my bets. Be careful out there.

My picks were 1-2 in Week 7 and I'm 12-9 on the season. I'm looking to get back in the win column this week.

Let's get to this week's picks. All lines and odds are from Fanduel and are as of Saturday, November 7.

The line for the game is Giants -3, with a total of 38.5.

Game Total OVER 38.5 (-115). This game scares me and while I think the Giants will win, I don't want to lay the points or take the moneyline, given all the bad history of their visits to this building. I do like the over, even though Philly's offense has been atrocious and hasn't topped 13 points in three straight. Milton and more Barkley should give them a spark. Philly's defense has a lot of talent and might give McCarthy some problems, but New York has been scoring and should put up points. This is also a make-or-break game for the Eagles' chances to stay in the race in a weak NFC East, and I think they'll compete. Weather won't be a factor. This game offers the lowest total on the board this week — grab the over.

Devin Singletary OVER 55.5 rushing yards (-114). This is a volume play, and my favorite prop on the board for the entire slate. The Giants are going to want to run the ball, and with Tyrone Tracy, Jr. on IR and Cam Skattebo doubtful, Singletary should get a major workload. They don't have any other backs on the roster with a rushing attempt this season. The Eagles are allowing 131 rushing yards per game which is the fifth-most in the NFL. A lot of that is game script, but this is a favorable matchup regardless. Singletary is competent enough to hit this prop by the end of the third quarter at the latest.

Joe Milton III UNDER 189.5 passing yards (-114). Saquon Barkley played just 19 snaps each of the last two weeks while dealing with injuries. I'm guessing he'll get a bigger workload this week, to help take pressure off Milton in his first start. DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks are out for the third straight game, so Philadelphia is once again going to need to rely on tight end Dallas Goedert plus a bunch of unproven and inexperienced pass catchers. Milton has a big arm but struggles with accuracy, and the Giants' pass defense has been playing really well. They held C.J. Stroud to just 153 yards in a game that went to overtime in Week 7. Tyler Shough only threw for 144 yards against them the week before. Take the under.

Those are the picks for Week 9. Good luck with your wagers! And if you play fantasy football, be sure to check out my Week 9 fantasy preview, with start/sit advice and more, right here.

#2940. Anthropic IPO meets AI backlash after midterms show stinging rebuke of data centers

Americans were unequivocal at the ballot box this week as they expressed disdain for tech billionaires and mistrust of artificial intelligence. With that backdrop, Anthropic is about to try to go public.

The AI company is gearing up for a potentially historic IPO, likely targeting mid-November for its Nasdaq debut. CNBC reported on Friday that Anthropic investors see the company aiming for a valuation of $1 trillion to $1.5 trillion, down from a target of $2 trillion that had been floated in recent months.

From Wall Street to Main Street, it's becoming a tougher environment for Anthropic. The Nasdaq on Thursday suffered its steepest drop in 19 months, and it's down to start November after October marked its worst month since March 2025. Chipmakers and neoclouds, the companies most closely tied to the AI buildout, are getting hit the hardest.

Voters rebuked AI in Tuesday's midterm elections, while sending Democrats to sweeping congressional victories across the country. Candidates who advocated for more stringent AI regulation won handily in states like Michigan, Ohio, Texas and Georgia, which will all now have Democratic governors, and local communities opposed data center-related ballot measures in 40 out of 41 cases.

More than half of voters said AI data centers were a "crucial" or "major factor" in who they supported for Congress, according to an exit poll from NBC News.

"People don't want a data center going near them if it's just going to house AI that's going to help a big tech company profit," Maura Austin, a behavioral scientist at the University of Virginia, told CNBC. "What Anthropic is doing when they're going public with such a high valuation is they're really leaning into that big tech, billionaire kind of caricature. I expect that's not going to go well with the public."

Anthropic has long tried to position itself as a safety-conscious company that takes AI's potential risks and benefits seriously. CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company alongside a handful of researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, and it primarily sells its products and services to other businesses.

The company's enterprise focus has helped it avoid becoming the public face of AI, leaving that distinction to OpenAI and CEO Sam Altman. But with Anthropic poised to become the first pure-play AI company to hit the public market, minting a number of new billionaires and many more millionaires in the process, a heightened level of scrutiny is coming.

"Being a public company, just that by itself pushes you in the limelight," Reena Aggarwal, a professor of finance at Georgetown University, said in an interview. "And then AI is such a hot-button issue that Anthropic will be in the limelight even more."

Matt Kennedy, a senior IPO market strategist at Renaissance Capital, said that's not necessarily a bad thing for Anthropic.

Public markets force greater transparency, he said, and the company could attract new users through its debut. And since AI has become such a mainstream conversation topic, several private companies are already household names.

"I would say that a public company does not deserve more criticisms than a comparable private company," Kennedy said. "So Anthropic should not be put in a harsher public spotlight than OpenAI just because it's public."

AI backlash accelerated over the summer after a series of high-profile safety and security incidents rattled the public. As concerns mounted, the debate over data centers intensified, while tech's hyperscalers simultaneously boosted their forecasts for capital expenditures, each reaching into the hundreds of billions of dollars.

Anthropic and OpenAI depend on state-of-the-art data centers to train models and serve customers, and they're trying to build out capacity as quickly as possible to meet demand. Anthropic said in an amended IPO prospectus this week that it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

Americans grappling with inflation and stubbornly high gas prices have grown worried that the facilities could exacerbate environmental harms, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss. For many, a proposed data center in their backyard became the physical manifestation of the widespread antipathy toward AI.

In the risk factors section of its prospectus, Anthropic said opposition to data center development has become more organized across jurisdictions, and that the company's public profile could draw particular scrutiny to projects associated with it.

Nicole Greene, an analyst at Gartner, said she expects that Anthropic's executives have long been prepared to contend with AI backlash, especially given the disruptive nature of the technology.

"There's going to be big emotions," Greene told CNBC. "With big emotions and feelings — fear and excitement — comes heightened scrutiny."

Amodei acknowledged the souring sentiment around AI in a post on X in August. He said it's the result of a "crisis of trust," and that people believe companies, the government and the tech industry are always "cooking up some new way to screw them over."

Rebuilding public confidence is going to be an uphill battle as the company begins providing quarterly financial updates.

The University of Southern California published a survey on Monday that found that 71% of respondents don't trust big tech companies to act in society's best interest "at all" around data center development.

"Once you've lost trust, it's hard to gain it back," Wändi Bruine de Bruin, a professor at the University of Southern California's Schaeffer Institute, said in an interview. "The numbers are not good."

#2939. How a third-quarter run amplified the Lakers’ resiliency

LOS ANGELES — Luka Dončić stepped up to the free throw line in the third quarter.

The Lakers' NBA Cup court plastered in gold. The crowd an ocean of the same color. Dončić was engulfed, but instead of the sound of waves flooding the senses, it was the MVP chants, each growing louder than the next.

Dončić made his first, marking his 40th point of the game and 20th in the quarter, capping off a 34-15 run in the frame that brought Los Angeles back from a 16-point deficit and gave them an 87-75 lead over the Portland Trail Blazers.

It was a run that put the Lakers in front heading into the final frame and helped them exact revenge on a Blazers side that handed them one of their two losses as they won 124-118 on Friday.

"Anytime you start a half down, you want to get to momentum early," head coach JJ Redick said of the third quarter. "We were playing from behind the first half, so I thought it was really important to get off to a good start, and we did that. … for the most part, it felt like we had control of the rest of the game, and a lot of that had to do with how we defended in the third quarter."

It's impossible to gloss over Dončić in this game, who finished with 53 points on 17-of-33 shooting from the field, passing Kareem Abdul-Jabbar for the 10th-most 50-point games in NBA history. The Lakers made their run and took control in the third quarter, but the momentum shifted when Dončić drained a shot from the opposite 3-point line as time expired in the first half.

"[That shot] changes the whole energy," Dončić said postgame. "In the team and in the gym. That was an important shot to make. So that's why I decided to make it."

At the time, the Lakers had made just three of their 23 3-point attempts and were enduring what looked like the game where the offense inevitably regressed to the mean. Instead, including Dončić's heave, Los Angeles made eight of their last 14 deep shots en route to a much-needed win.

Redick has preached patience and steady incline as a team all year, but a win like this for sole position at the top of the Western Conference — and on NBA Cup night no less — was a clear measure of their resilience.

And yet, Redick thinks they're still building.

"I think we're already a really together group," he added Friday. "You don't become a resilient team in 10 games, five games, 20 games. It takes a full season to build that resiliency. … But, the fact that this is a very together group and we've already shown a level of resiliency, it's exciting, it's promising and I want to continue building on that."

Much of this lands on Dončić's shoulders. He's having a historical season, one of the best we've ever seen, but he's a product of the team around him. It's a roster he had his hands all over, collectively crafted around his needs at every corner, a point driven by Portland head coach Micah Nori before they clashed on Friday.

Nori says it's Dončić's MVP to lose, "hands down," but because of everything around him. The shooting, the defending, Walker Kessler's rolling. It's all working in unison.

The Lakers have 72 games to go. It's not always going to be like this. But again, a night like Friday works wonders for the marathon Redick is trying to run.

"We all want good results," he said. "This is going to be a long process and it's going to take time for us to become a great basketball team. I like that our guys have responded to some adversity. Tonight was a great example, where we get down pretty big early and we just stick with it."

You can follow Connor on Twitter at @cmorenoNBA.

#2938. Berkshire Hathaway more than doubles buybacks in third quarter as earnings decline

Berkshire Hathaway said Saturday it accelerated its share repurchases during the third quarter, as operating and overall profits took a hit.

The Greg Abel-led conglomerate bought back $9.6 billion worth of Berkshire stock. That's more than double the $4.5 billion repurchased in the second quarter and brings the company's total buybacks for 2026 to about $14.4 billion.

The sharp increase came as Berkshire's operating earnings — those from wholly-owned businesses — fell year over year, as profits from its insurance business pulled back.

Berkshire operating profits totaled $12.705 billion, down 5.8% from $13.485 billion in the year-earlier period. Insurance underwriting tumbled 43% year on year to $1.352 billion, while insurance investment income declined 14% to $2.734 billion. Berkshire's railroad, energy and manufacturing businesses all saw earnings grow year over year.

The company's overall earnings, which include Berkshire's sprawling stock market portfolio, also dropped 28% in the third quarter to $22.112 billion from $30.796 billion a year prior. Investment gains for the company fell nearly 50% to $9.407 billion.

Despite the earnings pullback, Berkshire stock has outperformed recently amid a massive rotation out of technology stocks. Class A shares are up more than 12% over the past month, while the S&P 500 has dropped 5.5% in that time. Year to date, Berkshire has gained 13%, while the broad market index is up just 7.8%.

Berkshire's five largest equity holdings remained the same in the third quarter: Alphabet, American Express, Apple, Bank of America and Coca-Cola.

Berkshire's cash hoard also grew 3% in the third quarter $377.435 billion from $365.514 billion in the second quarter. However, excluding rail and utility cash along with T-bill payables, the conglomerate's cash holdings totaled $352.3 billion, down 1.9% quarter over quarter.

— CNBC's Alex Crippen contributed reporting.

#2937. The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

#2936. What makes this 76ers team stacked with superstars so unique

What makes this 76ers team stacked with superstars so unique originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

They're a team of superstars that don't play superstar basketball.

That's the beauty of this 76ers team. They play a brand of unselfish team-first basketball you rarely see from a team loaded with stars.

LeBron James is one of the two or three greatest players in NBA history, a four-time MVP, a four-time NBA champion, a 1st-ballot Hall of Famer.

Joel Embiid is a seven-time all-star, a former MVP, an Olympic gold medalist and a likely Hall of Famer.

Jaylen Brown is a five-time all-star, an NBA champion, a career 20 points-per-game scorer.

Tyrese Maxey is a two-time all-star, one of only two NBA guards averaging 27.0 points per game over the last four years.

Four certified megastars on one team. And somehow they've figured out how to leave their egos at the front door and play smart, tough, disciplined team basketball, the style of ball you just never see on these superstar-laden teams.

On offense, they patiently work the ball around, always looking for the open man or a positive matchup. You've got this bunch of big-name, high-priced All-Stars who are just as happy to pass the ball as shoot it. That never happens.

The 76ers are sixth in the NBA in assists, and they're one of only four teams with twice as many assists as turnovers. Smart, unselfish, patient, disciplined.

When an all-timer like LeBron averages 7.3 assists per game that speaks volumes. LeBron is fifth in the NBA in assists per game among forwards, which is wild. You can see the joy on his face when one of his passes leads to a basket. When one of the greatest players in history has that mentality, it rubs off on everyone around him.

Embiid hasn't really got going yet on offense and still takes too many 3's, but he's also embraced the team-first mentality, and his 4.8 assists per game are more than one full assist above his career average and 4th-most in the NBA among centers or forward-centers. And still one of the most dominating defensive players in the league.

Brown is averaging 27.0 points, which you expect, but he's also averaging 4.3 assists per game, significantly above his career average of 3.8 coming into the season. He's fit in so well so fast you forget its his first season here.

And what can you even say about Maxey? Just a beast. He's averaging nearly 30 points per game but never wastes a possession, always makes the right decision with the ball in his hands and is still finding time to average 4.3 assists per game.

With James, Embiid, Brown and Maxey, the 76ers are the only NBA team with four guys averaging at least 16 points and 4 assists per game. Only the Pacers, Magic and Raptors have three.

But where we're really seeing this group playing out of their minds is on the defensive end, where this bunch of all-stars, including two newcomers, has been astounding.

The 76ers have the 2nd-best defensive efficiency rating in the league, behind only the Pistons, who they play Wednesday night, and they're allowing the 2nd-fewest points in the league, also behind Detroit. Last year, they allowed 123 points per game through eight games. This year they're at 111.

They're holding teams to 43 percent shooting from the field, 3rd-best in the league and their lowest since 2011-12. Their 9.3 steals per game are 6th-most.

All five starters are among the top 25 players in the league in defensive rating – James is fourth, Embiid 11th, Edgecombe 15th, Maxey 22nd and Brown 23rd. How is that even possible?

It's rare to see superstar-caliber guys play as hard on the defensive end as the offensive end. Everybody on this team embraces it. They hustle, they work, they communicate and most importantly they commit to it.

The biggest concern when the 76ers built this dream team was that it would be a team of individuals out for themselves, but the reality has been the opposite.

In a very short period of time, they've learned how to play together. How to play for each other.

And we haven't even mentioned V.J. Edgecomb, who at 21 years old has figured out how to assimilate into this loaded lineup, never force anything, wait for his opportunities and make huge shots when he gets his turn. Edgecombe is at 12.7 points and 3.0 assists per game, and his numbers are understandably down a bit from last year, but if that bothers him it sure doesn't show.

And the role players – Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, Jabari Walker – have each adopted the same ego-free style of basketball, supporting the starters however they need to.

Nick Nurse deserves a ton of credit for getting this bunch to play this brand of hoops. It's not easy to get any NBA player to put team goals ahead of individual goals. But he's got Hall of Famers and all-stars doing it and having a blast along the way. They all seem to love playing basketball the right way and they seem to love playing together.

The 76ers are 7-1 and looking like a real threat to win the East, and they're doing it by playing the game the right way.

By committing to each other and not themselves.

#2935. Trump offers UFC chief Dana White press secretary job

President Donald Trump offered UFC president and CEO Dana White the job of press secretary on Saturday.

In a post on Truth Social, Trump said: "I am pleased to announce that the Great Dana White, a violent Fighter and Warrior, has been offered the job of Press Secretary of the United States."

"We need these people to defend us against the Lunatic Left," Trump wrote. "Dana will be a Press Secretary like none other. Dana, please take the job!"

Apparently in response to Trump's post, White posted a picture of himself on Instagram stories standing behind the press secretary's lectern in the White House, with the caption, "On to the next chapter."

It was unclear whether White has accepted the position or is simply acknowledging the president's request in jest.

Trump said that he was offering White the job of "Press Secretary of the United States," but the president's spokesperson is called the "White House press secretary." Trump also recently speculated about considering Trump loyalist and rapper Nicki Minaj as White House press secretary, in an interview with podcaster Benny Johnson.

Trump said the job offer to White extended to his assistants, "even though some are not from our Country." The so-called assistants Trump named included current and former UFC mixed martial arts fighters from countries such as Brazil and Russia.

In October, White House press secretary Katie Zacharia left the press secretary position, just five days after her stint as Karoline Leavitt's replacement began.

The president told reporters he suggested Zacharia quit after her first and only press briefing, where, in her six minutes answering questions behind the lectern, she only took questions from three conservative outlets.

"I just spoke to her about it. I said, 'Katie, is this for you?'" Trump said outside the White House on Oct. 16.

The president's appeal to White follows Friday's announcement that Trump's deputy chief of staff, Stephen Miller, would be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump's stunning shake-up comes just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate.

The president and White have a longstanding friendship. During Trump's first campaign in 2016, White praised the then-Republican nominee and called him a "fighter."

"Nobody took us seriously. Nobody. Except Donald Trump. Donald was the first guy to recognize the potential we saw in the UFC and encouraged us to build our business," White said.

And for his 80th birthday in June, President Trump hosted a UFC match — dubbed UFC Freedom 250 — on the White House's South Lawn.

The White House and the UFC didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#2934. Risk of Iran war escalation remains high despite Democratic sweep in U.S. midterm elections

The U.S. and Iran are careening toward renewed war despite growing hopes this week that the Democrats' decisive midterm election victory will bring the conflict to an end, analysts say.

Crude oil prices fell more than 16% over the last five trading days after Vice President JD Vance publicly blamed the Republicans' stinging loss on the war. Vance's remarks raised questions about whether there is still support within the administration to continue the conflict with Iran.

The Democrats plan to use their comfortable majorities in both chambers of Congress to pass a war powers resolution that demands President Donald Trump end the war. But tensions in the Middle East have not eased since the election, and the Democrats aren't sworn in until January, the analysts say.

"Throughout the week, we were struck by the divergence between deepening escalation concerns in the Gulf and the headline driven off-ramp optimism of market participants thousands of miles away," Helima Croft, head of global commodity strategy at RBC Capital Markets, told investors in a note Friday.

Several oil tankers have come under attack near Oman since the election, killing one seafarer, according to maritime security agencies. Iran's Houthi allies in Yemen continue to fire missiles at neighboring Saudi Arabia.

Meanwhile, the U.S. military buildup continues, with a third carrier strike group en route to the region. The consulting group Rapidan Energy sees an 80% chance that fighting will resume as diplomacy stalls.

President Donald Trump told reporters Thursday that he will make a decision soon on whether to attack Iran again.

"I would rather not destroy the rest of the country if possible but maybe I'll have to," Trump said in the Oval Office. "You'll know soon enough."

"We're locked and loaded," the president said. "If we have to do the rest of the Iran thing, we're very much locked in."

Crude oil prices will vault above $100 a barrel again in response to renewed fighting, said Bob McNally, president of Rapidan and a former energy advisor to President George W. Bush. Brent crude, the international benchmark, was trading above $97 on Friday while U.S. crude was around $90.

"President Donald Trump is determined as a legacy item, almost at all costs, to prevent Iran from getting a nuclear weapon, having them give up their nuclear capabilities," McNally told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Friday.

"The Iranian regime, on the other hand, is not willing to do it," the analyst said. The escalation dynamic remains in place so long as that core difference between the U.S. and Iran persists, he said.

The risk of war is still alive, especially since Trump doesn't face anymore future elections, said Amos Hochstein, a former energy advisor to President Joe Biden. Gasoline, diesel and jet fuel prices would all surge as a consequence, Hochstein told CNBC's "Power Lunch" Friday.

Tehran has been taunting the Trump administration since the election. The social media account of Iran's Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei said the "process of the U.S.'s collapse has accelerated" after the midterm results.

And Congress is unlikely to pass a war powers resolution until Democrats take control in January. Trump can veto such a resolution in any case.

The main leverage Congress will have is the power of the purse, Croft said. Government funding expires on Dec. 11 and negotiations for another continuing resolution could drag into 2027, she said. This would give Democrats more leverage when they take power in January, the analyst said.

"Importantly, deferring funding decisions would not immediately constrain Trump's ability to conduct military operations, but could significantly reshape the administration's longer-term funding outlook," Croft told investors.

Trump discussed diplomatic efforts to de-escalate tensions in the region with the emir of Qatar during a Friday phone call, according to Qatar's state news agency.

An unnamed senior Iranian official told Reuters on Thursday that Tehran sent a nuclear proposal to the U.S. through Qatar. Tehran will only discuss the proposal after the U.S. meets its demands, the Iranian official told Reuters.

Trump seemed to dismiss the diplomatic efforts Thursday.

"Iran will never have a nuclear weapon," the president said. "They're not doing anything without our approval. It doesn't make any difference what they're looking at," he said of the diplomatic efforts.

#2933. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2929. Sixers-Cavs by the numbers: Pressure, threes and a 29-point rematch

Sixers-Cavs by the numbers: Pressure, threes and a 29-point rematch originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers and Cavaliers have already played one game that looked like two.

Cleveland led by 29 points during the Sixers' home opener on Oct. 22 and carried a 69-45 advantage into halftime. Philadelphia responded by winning the second half, 69-37, and completing a 114-106 comeback.

It was the Sixers' largest comeback since the NBA began maintaining detailed play-by-play data in 1997-98. Only nine other teams during that span had overcome a deficit of at least 29 points.

Fifteen days later, the teams will meet again Friday night at Rocket Arena. This time, the matchup counts toward the NBA Cup standings.

The 8-1 Sixers have won five consecutive games and opened group play with a 98-93 victory over the Knicks. The 4-4 Cavs have dropped two straight and will make their Cup debut.

Here are five numbers that help frame the rematch.

Philadelphia's comeback required more than a hot shooting stretch.

The Sixers committed 15 turnovers in the first half, allowing Cleveland to build a 22-6 advantage in points off turnovers. Their pressure then changed the game after halftime.

The Cavs committed 21 turnovers by the end of the night. What had been a 16-point Cleveland advantage in points off turnovers finished even at 22 apiece.

"They upped their pressure and physicality," Cavs head coach Kenny Atkinson said after the game. "It was pretty clear. We did not handle it well."

The Sixers scored 64 of their 69 second-half points through their starting lineup. Tyrese Maxey finished with 28 points, and Philadelphia turned a game that appeared finished into a franchise-record comeback.

The first half of the opening meeting now looks like an exception.

The Sixers have committed 12.9 turnovers per 100 possessions this season, the NBA's second-lowest rate. They are also third in turnover differential, averaging 3.4 fewer giveaways than their opponents.

That is notable for a team that entered the season intending to play faster. Philadelphia has pushed the ball without routinely surrendering possessions.

LeBron James' presence as the primary facilitator has helped. So has Maxey's willingness to move the ball when defenses trap him. Joel Embiid has averaged 5.2 assists, third among qualified centers, and the Sixers have increasingly trusted VJ Edgecombe to make decisions late in games.

Cleveland's defense has not forced many mistakes. The Cavs enter Friday near the bottom of the league in opponent turnover rate.

The first meeting swung when the Sixers stopped giving away possessions and forced Cleveland into mistakes of its own.

The Sixers have allowed 105.1 points per 100 possessions, the NBA's second-best defensive rating. Cleveland enters with the league's ninth-ranked offense.

The Cavs are the only top-10 offense the Sixers have faced through their first nine games.

Philadelphia has held opponents to a 50 percent effective field-goal percentage and 31.8 percent shooting from three-point range. Its defensive shot profile has been encouraging, too. Only 46 percent of opposing three-point attempts have been classified as wide open, the fifth-lowest share in the league, and opponents have taken just 22 percent of their threes from the corners.

There is reason for caution. Based on the quality of shots Philadelphia has allowed, only Washington's opponents have underperformed their expected effective field-goal percentage by a wider margin. The Sixers have defended well, but opponents are unlikely to remain this cold indefinitely.

Cleveland, with Donovan Mitchell, James Harden and Evan Mobley, can test whether those numbers hold against high-level individual shot creation.

The Cavs have attempted exactly 42 three-pointers per game and made 37.2 percent of them.

Cleveland went 7 for 29 from beyond the arc in Philadelphia. Mitchell, Harden and De'Andre Hunter combined to shoot 3 for 17. The Cavs scored 60 points in the paint and still reached 106, but their 24.1 percent performance from three prevented them from stopping the Sixers' comeback.

Friday's game could look considerably different if Cleveland generates its normal volume.

The Sixers have controlled the three-point line well so far, but Mitchell is coming off a 27-point performance against Oklahoma City and scored 32 in the first meeting with Philadelphia. Harden added 16 points that night, although his six turnovers became costly as the Sixers increased their pressure.

Philadelphia has been outrebounded by 5.1 per game. The Cavs rank sixth with 47.5 rebounds per contest.

The Sixers have already shown they can survive a major deficit on the glass. They beat Milwaukee by 11 despite getting outrebounded by 22. That is not a formula they will want to test regularly against Cleveland's size.

Mobley had 18 points and eight rebounds in the first meeting. Jarrett Allen added 14 points and seven boards, helping Cleveland win the rebounding battle, 46-44.

Whether Allen plays Friday remains uncertain.

Cleveland listed Allen as questionable because of a right knee injury after he left Wednesday's loss to Oklahoma City in the first quarter. Allen collided knee-to-knee with Thunder forward Chet Holmgren and did not return.

The Cavs also listed Peyton Watson as questionable with right hamstring soreness and Tyrese Proctor as questionable with an ankle injury. Proctor left Wednesday's game during the fourth quarter. Jaylon Tyson is out with a right ankle sprain.

Allen's availability could significantly alter the matchup on the glass. If he sits, Thomas Bryant would likely handle more of the center minutes behind Mobley. If he plays, Cleveland will have the same starting frontcourt that outrebounded the Sixers during the first meeting.

Atkinson has emphasized the possession battle during Cleveland's two-game slide.

"You've got to get more shots than the other team," he said this week. "Rebounding is a big part of it."

That also carries added importance in the NBA Cup, where point differential is the second tiebreaker after head-to-head results.

Philadelphia enters Friday at 1-0 with a plus-five differential in East Group B. Miami is also 1-0 at plus-20, while Cleveland is playing its first group game.

The Sixers already know they can erase 29 points against the Cavs. A cleaner start would be far more useful than proving it twice.

#2927. Novorizontino first to secure promotion to the 2027 Brasileirão

Grêmio Novorizontino are the first club to secure promotion from this year's Série B.

Their long-awaited place in the 2027 Brasileirão was secured this Saturday (7) with a comeback win over Fortaleza in the penultimate round of the second division.

It will be the first time the club from the interior of São Paulo state have played in the top flight of Brazilian football.

This year's São Paulo state championship runners-up, Novorizontino had come close in recent editions of Série B.

In 2023 and 2024, the team finished fifth, one place below the promotion spots.

Last year, a run of poor results in the closing stages meant their dream was put on hold once again.

If Juventude fail to win away at Cuiabá this Saturday, Novorizontino will also clinch the 2026 Série B title.

Only the top two teams in this year's Série B earn automatic promotion to the first division.

The clubs finishing third to sixth will compete in playoffs to decide the other two promotion places.

Fortaleza are still in the hunt for promotion, but Saturday's defeat ended their chances of finishing in the top two.

#2926. Raiders Mailbag: Should Las Vegas consider trading Tre Tucker?

The Week 9 matchup against the San Francisco 49ers marks the halfway point of the Las Vegas Raiders' 2026 season, and the NFL trade deadline is coming up on Tuesday. So, we have a lot to go over for this week's mailbag. Let's get to it!

A: It depends on how they feel about re-signing Tre Tucker in the offseason. If John Spytek and Co. don't want to prioritize Tucker, then it would make sense to get something for him, especially for a Top 100 pick. However, the Raiders are still in the hunt, so I don't think they're going to be looking to move on from starters at the deadline.

As far as re-signing Tucker goes, I don't think Jalen Nailor's contract impacts that much. Tucker has already out-produced Nailor, with almost double the amount of yards (2,040 to 1,179) and catches (152 to 81) and has more touchdowns (14 to 12) despite being in the NFL for one fewer season. Nailor is making less than $12 million per year (ranks 40th among WRs, per Over The Cap), and I'd bet Tucker gets something similar to Jakobi Meyer's contract with the Jaguars ($20 million per year, 27th among WRs).

Also, it's not ideal, but the Raiders can get out of Nailor's contract if that becomes an issue in the offseason. Per OTC, they'd save $5.5 million and take on about $9.4 million in dead cap with a post-June 1 cut, and it's about a 50/50 split with $7.4 million in savings via a pre-June 1 trade, and a little more than $12 million in savings with a post-June 1 trade. Again, not an ideal situation, but there are options available.

Personally, I feel like the need for a 'WR1' is kind of diminishing by the week. The offense and passing game are functioning pretty well with Brock Bowers as the top option, while the team is struggling to find answers at guard and defensive tackle. As long as Bowers is around, I wouldn't mind rolling with a collection of cheaper 'WR2s' like Tucker and allocating more resources to the trenches.

However, then the issue becomes: When and how much does Bowers want to get paid? He's going to be eligible for a contract extension in the offseason and will reset the tight end market whenever he gets his second contract. Plus, the offense's current 'WR1' will probably look to get 'WR1' money from the Raiders, which is in the $30-plus million per year range.

Q: Might there be any indication on how the Raiders will handle the trade deadline? Or do they have good poker faces?

A: Coincidentally, my power rankings roundup column had some trade deadline talk in it, where ESPN dove into the best in-season trade the Raiders have ever had (DB Mike Haynes in 1983). I was trying to think of the second-best in-season acquisition the franchise has made at the deadline, and had a hard time coming up with one. The reason I bring this up is that, historically, the Raiders don't make many moves at the deadline, at least not as a 'buyer'.

Considering that win or lose, they'll still be in the playoff hunt after this weekend, I have a hard time seeing Kirk Cousins getting traded. On top of that, the only significant trade rumor coming out of Las Vegas right now is that Maxx Crosby is not on the market. So, maybe a smaller deal like Powers-Johnson getting traded happens, but anything else would be a surprise at this point.

I get the feeling Spytek and Klint Kubiak are content to roll with the roster as is for the rest of the season and then address the needs in the offseason.

A: Again, I don't think we'll see any splash trades coming out of Vegas in the next few days. But to answer the question: Maxx Crosby, Kirk Cousins, Tucker (third-fourth fringe), Kolton Miller (third-fourth fringe), and Ashton Jeanty.

Crosby would have to be for a haul, and it sounds like the organization is trying to make it known that he isn't going anywhere. I touched on this in more detail last week; I'd hold onto Cousins and Miller to help support Fernando Mendoza. And I'll save y'all some time rather than rehash my Tucker and Jeanty takes. The running back will come up again later.

Q: Under Rob Leonard at defensive coordinator, Eric Stokes doesn't look as good as he did last year. At what point do they let all 3 young CBs play both CB spots? Did the league catch on to Rob Leonard's scheme, or why does the defense look so bad compared to the first 3 weeks?

A: The problem is that Darien Porter and Hezekiah Masses are both inconsistent right now, and Jermod McCoy's tape is pretty ugly on the limited snaps he's played this season. So, while Stokes has regressed, he's still probably the most reliable corner on the roster. As long as the Raiders are in the playoff mix, I don't think the coaching staff pivots to the full-on youth movement. But once they fall out of contention, then we could see Porter, Masses and McCoy get more opportunities.

As for Leonard's scheme, opposing offenses have definitely been picking on his tendacies in the last several weeks. The first two games had a lot of mystery about what he was going to run, making it difficult for the Dolphins and Chargers to put a typical game plan together. Also, we now know those are two of the worst teams in the league.

Even the Saints started to figure out Leonard, putting up 27 points in three quarters before turning the ball over on every possession in the fourth. New Orleans still racked up 381 yards, and that was our first sign that the book was out on the Raiders' defense.

The Chiefs were the first team to really highlight this; the Raiders can't stop the run consistently against 12 and 13 personnel. I dive into that in more detail when talking about the defensive tackles. I think part of it is a talent issue to cut Leonard some slack.

Ultimately, with the way it's currently constructed from scheme and personnel perspectives, this is a very turnover-reliant defense. That's hard to sustain because it requires the other team/offense to make mistakes.

Q: Why are the Raiders struggling to find a NT for their 3-4? Do they not have to correct players on the roster? Seems like they had numerous opportunities to grab one in the draft or through a trade. Yet they shuffle through players who don't seem to fit a true NT but are serviceable. Do they not hold NT an important position? Seems very neglectful by Spytek if your base is 3-4.

A: They didn't prioritize the position at all in the offseason. The starting nose tackle right now is Foley Fatukasi, who was signed after the first preseason game. As you mentioned, there were plenty of opportunities to address it in the offseason. This draft class had several good nose tackles, but Spytek went in a different direction every time.

I think that came down to two factors. One being that the front office wasn't going to be able to fill every need in one offseason. Think about how many starting spots were up for grabs or were questionable heading into training camp.

The other factor is that the 3-4 discussion was kind of a fallacy, where Rob Leonard ideally runs a variety of fronts. In the first few games of the season, the Raiders were in more even fronts out of nickel personnel. Then, teams started to realize the defense struggles to stop 12 and 13 personnel. Leonard likes to counter those looks by putting an off-ball linebacker (typically Quay Walker) on the line of scrimmage at edge to get into an odd front.

That's where not having a good nose tackle really started to show up because teams like the Chiefs, Bills and Rams just stayed in 12 and 13 personnel, forcing the Raiders to play fronts that they aren't adequately equipped to play.

So, I think it was a combination of negligence from Spytek and the defense not functioning the way Leonard envisioned in the offseason. Granted, you could argue that the former is the cause of the latter. It's certainly time for Spytek to place a bigger priority on the defensive tackle position as a whole than he has in the past.

Q: What's your take on Jonah Laulu getting waived & claimed by six teams versus the defensive tackle room, with Dont'e Thornton Jr. getting reactivated in his place and then inactive for the game?

A: I don't have a problem with Laulu getting waived. He was playing poorly, and defensive tackle was going to be a major offseason need even if he was still on the roster heading into the spring. But seeing six teams put in a claim for Laulu certainly feels like a missed opportunity to at least pick up a seventh-round pick instead of letting him go for nothing.

However, to play devil's advocate, just because a team is willing to claim a player for free doesn't necessarily mean they'd be willing to give up anything for him, even if it is a small price tag. Look at Darrell Luter Jr. The Raiders were one of five teams to put a claim in for him after cutdown day.

Maybe the 49ers didn't try or think they could get anything for Luter, but that's a highly functional organization that was in a similar situation to the Raiders with Laulu, and both clubs handled it the same way. Meanwhile, Luter has strictly been a special teamer in Las Vegas, and people would probably be pissed if they gave up a draft pick for that.

Leonard tried the 5-2-4 looks with Tommy Eichenberg serving as the base package linebacker against the Rams. Vegas was in base personnel 61.8% of that game, per Sumer Sports, which is about 40% higher than the rate for the entire season. But it didn't matter, as Los Angeles still ran the ball for 200 yards. However, Eichenberg had a very good game last week and was a big reason the defense got stops and was better against the run in the red zone.

So, we have conflicting information on the topic. But I wouldn't be surprised if Eichenberg gets another shot at having a bigger role this Sunday considering how well he played in New York. It's not like the Raiders are keeping points off the board in nickel!

Q: Will Fernando Mendoza start this year? Is it coaching and organizational malpractice to not play him?

A: Call me a pessimist, but I have a hard time seeing the Raiders winning more than one of their next four games (at 49ers, vs. Seahawks, at Broncos and at Browns). Granted, I think they can pull off an upset in Cleveland. However, at 5-7 heading into December, it'd be stretching the definition of "in the hunt" to consider Vegas a legitimate playoff contender at that point. So, I think there's a good chance Mendoza's first start comes after the bye in Week 14.

I won't go as far as saying it'd be "malpractice" not to play him this year, but I will say that they need to take the training wheels off at some point and let him get live reps. It might not be pretty, but let him work through the struggles now and have something to build on heading into 2027.

I understand why Klint Kubiak didn't do it, but I would have made the change ahead of last week's game. I get wanting to be patient and have been on board with that strategy from the jump. However, now that we're halfway through the season, making a quarterback change needs to be a bigger discussion within the organization, and sitting Mendoza for the entire campaign wouldn't be doing him any favors, in my opinion.

The Raiders still have a chance at making the playoffs right now, and Cousins is playing well. So there's no need to make a change right now. But once the postseason becomes an afterthought, it's time to turn it over to the rookie.

A: I think so. It's only been one game, but Trey Zuhn III has already played better than Caleb Rogers did in any of his starts this year, for example. Also, Masses is going back and forth with Porter despite being drafted two rounds later, and Mike Washington Jr. is pushing Ashton Jeanty for the lead running back role despite being drafted three rounds later.

The 2026 draft class may not be perfect, but it's already surpassing last year's, and the top pick has barely seen the field!

A: I think Jeanty's situation last week was a combination of both. The running game hasn't been working (that's the earth-shattering analysis you're here for, right?), and while it wasn't/isn't all his fault, Jeanty wasn't making it better.

Kubiak had to try something else and see what Washington could do as the feature back. However, I think the dramatic 25-2 split was to send a message to Jeanty and was partially punitive for his stupid 'bulletin board' comments about the Jets' defense.

Moving forward, I think it will be closer to a 50-50 workload between the two backs. The rushing attack was better at staying on schedule with Washington last week, but his lack of vision also led to some missed opportunities for big runs, and the Jets were down their best run defender, defensive tackle T'Vondre Sweat. In other words, the rookie had a decent performance to increase his workload from what it was earlier in the season, but he didn't run away with the job.

As for what Jeanty's future looks like in Las Vegas, that's a good question that I don't have a great answer for right now. Sunday could help give us more clarity. But if I'm calling the shots and another team was willing to give me, say, a second-round pick for Jeanty on Tuesday, I'd have a hard time turning that down. I don't think that will happen; however, depending on how the next couple of months play out, it could be a big topic in the offseason.

Q: Should Jackson Powers-Johnson be playing center instead of guard, or does he not have the skills to play in the Raiders system(s), or do the abilities that made him successful in college not translate to the NFL?

A: I've always felt like JPJ's best position is at center, skill-wise at least. I think the problem is that he wasn't able to get the mental part of the position down, which led to communication issues in the trenches. In other words, he was executing his assignment but wasn't getting the rest of the line on the same page, and the latter is half the battle for a center. It'd be like having a quarterback who has a strong arm but can't read defenses, anticipate throws, etc.

That's why I think the Raiders tried Powers-Johnson at guard, to let his physical tools flourish and take the thinking off his plate. But, for whatever reason, it hasn't worked out. Some guys can make that transition naturally; others just never feel comfortable not being at the pivot.

The other problem is once Vegas gave Tyler Linderbaum $81 million in the offseason, that ended any ideas of JPJ playing center moving forward. Linderbaum hasn't lived up to the contract, but he's been better than JPJ has and has the superior resume of the two. That's why I've been saying since his benching that Jackson Powers-Johnson's days in Vegas are likely numbered.

A: I feel like that's pretty spot on. Based on what we've seen so far, I think this year's Raiders are a frisky, seven- to eight-win club. Not quite a playoff team, but floating around an average ranking. Seeing as I thought they'd only win five games heading into the season, that's a good starting point for a rebuild.

That'll do it for this week's mailbag. The latter will continue to publish on Thursdays.

#2922. Desmond Bane ruled out as Magic down another starter at Warriors

Already without starters Paolo Banchero (right high ankle sprain) and Jalen Suggs (right knee bone bruise) entering Saturday's game at Golden State, the Magic received more negative news regarding player availability.

Roughly six hours ahead of tip-off against the Warriors inside Chase Center, the Magic added Desmond Bane to the team's injury report.

Bane, who appeared in and started all 82 regular-season games in his first season with Orlando last year, was ruled out of Saturday's contest because of an illness.

It marks the first regular-season game Bane will miss since April 13, 2025, when he was still with the Memphis Grizzlies. Last season with Orlando, Bane was one of only four players in the NBA to start all 82 regular-season games. He was the first Magic player to do that since Dwight Howard in the 2009-10 season.

Through the first eight games this season, his seventh year as a pro, Bane averaged 17.5 points, 3.9 rebounds and 2.3 assists during 34.5 minutes per night while shooting 36.8% from 3-point point range. Between last season and the start of this season, Bane had played in 90 consecutive regular-season games.

Entering Saturday's slate of games, Bane was tied for 17th league-wide in minutes played per contest, according to NBA.com. His teammate Franz Wagner, who averaged 36.1 minutes per night ahead of Saturday, was fifth in the NBA.

In addition to Bane, Suggs and Banchero, the Magic were also missing forwards Noah Penda (partially torn right hamstring), Jonathan Isaac (left knee injury management) and two-way rookie Izaiyah Nelson (left ankle injury recovery) at Golden State.

Those absences left Magic coach Sean Sweeney with only nine standard contract players available and a pair of two-way players as well against the Warriors, who entered Saturday on a six-game winning streak.

Orlando still has a standard contract roster spot open but Magic president of basketball operations Jeff Weltman said at the team's media day Sept. 28 that there were no immediate plans to fill it — largely due to the team's lack of financial flexibility.

The Magic continue their four-game road trip Tuesday night at Sacramento in the first contest of a back-to-back. Orlando travels to Utah the following night before returning to Kia Center to host the Bucks in NBA Cup action Friday.

#2920. SB Nation Reacts Results: Ch-Ch-Changes

We've got our SB Nation Reacts results for this week ready to roll, so let's take a look at what fans of the Minnesota Vikings have to say about our favorite team this week, shall we?

Our first question for this week, as it always is, has to do with whether or not everyone is confident with the direction of the team. And, coming after the first loss of the season in Week 8, the answer is a resounding, "No. . .no, we are not."

Of those who responded to our poll for this week, just 17% are confident that a team currently sporting a 6-1 record is moving in the right direction. On the surface, that sounds a bit absurd, but honestly. . .it's not terribly difficult to understand. The offense, once again, struggled to score against a defense that it really shouldn't have struggled against, and the defense eventually just wore down as the offense wasn't able to stay on the field. It's the same formula that we've seen repeatedly this season, and something has to change going forward.

Which leads us to our second question for a week, which asked about whether or not the team needed to make a change at quarterback. Now, keep in mind that we posted this question before the team formally made the announcement that Carson Wentz would start at quarterback against the Buffalo Bills on Monday, but I'm not sure how much it would have affected the results.

Of those who responded to our poll, 78% agreed that a change needed to be made at quarterback in some form. There were 45% who said that the Vikings should go to Wentz, which is what has happened. . .so far. Another 33% believe that the Vikings should make a trade, which is something that could still happen before the NFL trade deadline, which arrives this Tuesday at 3:00 PM Central Time. The other 22% believe that the Vikings should stay the course at quarterback, which simply tells me that there were more Bears, Lions, and Packers fans voting in the poll than usual this week.

As a bit of a bonus question this week, GA Skol has put a poll over in the feed about whether or not the shift to Wentz gives the Vikings a better chance to win on Monday night against the Bills.

Those are our SB Nation Reacts results for this week, ladies and gentlemen! Thank you, as always, to everyone who took the time to participate in this week's polls, and keep your eyes out for next week's questions!

#2918. Observations after Sixers beat Bulls to reach East-leading 7-1

Observations after Sixers beat Bulls to reach East-leading 7-1 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers kept up their hot start to the season Monday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, reaching 7-1 with a 126-107 win against the Bulls.

All five of the Sixers' starters scored in double figures. Tyrese Maxey posted 26 points on 10-for-15 shooting and 11 assists. Jaylen Brown tallied a season-best 25 points and 11 rebounds.

VJ Edgecombe's 21 points were a new season high. LeBron James recorded 15 points, nine assists and seven boards.

Bulls rookie Caleb Wilson had 24 points and seven rebounds.

Joel Embiid (15 points, eight rebounds) had been listed as questionable with an official designation of "right foot plantar fascia injury management." He sat out Saturday on the second night of a back-to-back for the Sixers' win over the Hornets.

"Plantar is a little bit of a come and go type of thing," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse told reporters pregame. "They're just trying to manage it. It's very painful, if you've ever had it. It can kind of come and go out of nowhere. So we're just trying to manage the pain with that, be as smart as we can."

Nurse told reporters that Dominick Barlow is set to see a specialist Tuesday about the back injury that's sidelined him to start the season.

"He's been on court a little bit … not a ton of moving around," Nurse said. "I think they want to get to tomorrow's appointment. Hopefully, it'll be positive and we can get into his workouts a little bit."

The Sixers will begin a three-game road trip Wednesday when they visit the Pistons. Here are observations on their victory Monday:

After Embiid blitzed a Bulls pick-and-roll, Maxey picked off Matas Buzelis' hurried pass to get the Sixers rolling in transition and James jammed home the team's first field goal.

Edgecombe got a slam soon after James grabbed a defensive rebound and the Sixers again pushed the pace. The Sixers scored six quick fast-break points, leading the Bulls to burn an early timeout, and James' long-distance feeds were a major asset.

"If you're open, he's going to pass it," Edgecombe said of James. "The main thing is just being ready. Tonight I had to run and go get 'em. If you're open and he sees you, he's going to pass the ball."

Edgecombe had a dunk-heavy first quarter. He threw down his second when Dean Wade nabbed a steal and dropped off a typically selfless dish to the Sixers' explosive second-year guard, whose slam put the team up 22-13.

The Sixers have an abundance of dangerous options in the open floor, Edgecombe included. As he's already shown with his continued clutch shotmaking, the 21-year-old won't become anything resembling a non-factor offensively this year as a byproduct of the Sixers' enhanced star power.

Over the team's first seven games, Edgecombe had averaged 11.3 points. He scored 11 in the first period on 5-for-5 shooting.

Adem Bona missed a dunk early in the second quarter, which stung a bit more when Jalen Smith drilled a three-pointer seconds later.

Bona remained the Sixers' main backup center. He had a poor first-half stint overall amid a largely negative start to his third season. The 23-year-old big man played well in the Sixers' victory over the Bucks, posting six points, 13 rebounds and three blocks. Outside of that, his season has been sparse on bright moments so far. It's reasonable to wonder when Nurse might turn to alternatives like Jabari Walker, who played a good game Saturday in Charlotte, or try James again at the five.

The Bulls outscored the Sixers by eight points in Bona's first run, cutting their derficit to 40-38. Chicago went in front with a three by former Sixer Buddy Hield.

The Sixers regained the advantage late in the second quarter. Maxey and James each were excellent as passers in the first half, combining for 14 assists and three turnovers. James fired the ball to Wade for a corner three that gave the Sixers a 60-52 lead.

Following a third-quarter Embiid fadeaway jumper and two James lay-ins, the Sixers were up 17 points.

The Bulls cut the Sixers' lead down to 11 points by the start of the fourth quarter. With Bona still struggling and on four fouls, Wade began the fourth at center. Bona then briefly spelled Wade before being whistled for his fifth foul and taking a seat. Embiid ultimately wasn't needed for any of the fourth period and only logged 25 minutes.

Embiid said postgame that he was not available to return to the action in the fourth and has been feeling his plantar fasciitis on the court. The seven-time All-Star's health is clearly not close to 100 percent.

"I was on a minutes restriction," he said. "(The foot) has been going like that for the past few weeks. It's painful. Just got to manage it.

"If it flares up pretty bad, got to take a step back. We've been able to manage it, but it has its ups and downs."

The Sixers' lead got as low as eight points, but Chicago did not shoot well enough from three-point range (26.3 percent) or have sufficient firepower to sustain a meaningful comeback run. The bottom line is the Sixers are in a different class than the Bulls, who fell to 1-6.

As for the Sixers, they'll wake up Tuesday morning at the top of the Eastern Conference standings.

"You've got some of these nights where you kind of call them professional wins. …You look at the records and all that kind of stuff, but they have a lot of talent," Nurse said. "A lot of these teams play a way that's a little scary. It's fast and it's furious, lots of threes. … They're going to keep coming at you, high-possession games. So they're not easy games and I'm glad that we handled it professionally tonight."
